Chapter Notes
See the end of the chapter for notes
Silence pressed in around ***, dense and suffocating.
Then, from the distance, a faint hum reached her—light and joyous, a melody piercing
through the stillness. The sound grew louder, brighter, until a figure emerged, rushing toward
her through the darkness.
Sibling.
{REDACTED}
It glided through the void with effortless, uncontained energy. An amorphous silhouette
shimmering with vibrant life, its tentacles coiling and uncoiling in playful spirals, each
movement a small celebration against the emptiness.
Without hesitation, {REDACTED} began speaking, drifting closer with movements like
dancing. Its voice cascaded in quick, lively bursts—syllables threaded with strange
undertones that shifted and warped.
"...boring... always so... why won't you..."
The fragments reached *** like echoes through water. {REDACTED} spun in place,
tentacles flaring dramatically.
"...play... just once... come on..."
It darted forward, poking at ***'s form with a tentacle before retreating with what could only
be described as a giggle—if giggles could exist in the void.
Still nothing from ***. Just patient watching.
Yet *** felt no irritation toward {REDACTED}. Its presence pushed back the vast emptiness,
made the silence less absolute. That was enough.
{REDACTED} circled ***, never quite still, its energy a constant gentle pressure against the
void's weight. It tried new approaches—mimicking ***'s stillness for a moment before
bursting back into motion, modulating its incomprehensible voice through different tones,
even attempting to match the slow pulse of ***'s own faint luminescence.
After standing in that flow of futile communication, *** felt a familiar urge—a faint pull
drawing her elsewhere. Almost reflexively, she let her form drift.
The space bent around her, reality folding—And snapped back.
*** found itself exactly where it had been, as if the movement had never happened.
{REDACTED} watched with something like satisfaction, tentacles swaying in smug little
waves.
"...not letting you... drift away…fifth time…!”
The words were clearer this time, edged with playful authority. Time itself seemed to hiccup
around {REDACTED}, moments stuttering and rewinding in tiny loops.
"...always trying to... float off... so rude..."
*** blinked slowly. The sensation was strange—moving yet not moving, caught in
{REDACTED}'s temporal web.
Time stretched between them, silent and heavy. Then {REDACTED} spoke again, its tone
sharper now.
"...hate this... you used to... at least try..."
The melodious quality cracked, revealing frustration beneath.
"...remember when... and you would... but now..."
Another pause. Then a sound *** had never heard before—a huff of frustration, startlingly
human in its petulance.
"...fine... be that way... stupid..."
{REDACTED} turned its back, tentacles folding in an unmistakable sulk. But even in anger,
it couldn't stay still—tentacles tapping against the void in irritated rhythms, form wiggling
with barely contained energy.
Side by side yet worlds apart, they existed. *** felt no resentment. This was simply how
things were.
Together. Alone, but not quite.
Time passed in its meaningless way.
One day, distant figures appeared—vague shadows moving along their unknowable paths.
Outer Ones, vast and indifferent. Yet this time felt different. {REDACTED} stirred, turning
toward them with sudden interest.
"...finally... others who might... actually fun..."
Why is it looking at them?Unease flickered in ***'s depths. The Others usually ignored them, occasionally mocking as
they passed. Better to remain unnoticed.
But {REDACTED} continued watching, form practically vibrating with excitement. Its
tentacles reached out toward the distant figures, pulled back, reached again—a dance of
indecision.
"...going to... see what... maybe they'll..."
It turned to ***, and for a moment, the playful mask slipped.
"...you could... come with... if you wanted..."
But *** only stared, uncomprehending.
{REDACTED}'s form dimmed slightly. Then, forcing brightness back into its voice:
"...fine... stay here... boring void... boring idiot…”
{REDACTED} hesitated. Moved toward the figures. Stopped. Looked back at ***.
"...last chance... still time to... change your mind..."
Its form flickered with something desperate beneath the teasing. When *** remained
motionless, {REDACTED} made one final attempt:
"...promise I'll... come back... just wait... okay?"
Then, with visible effort, it turned away. Started moving toward the distant Others.
But every few moments, it looked back. Each glance lasting longer than the one before. At
the edge of ***'s perception, {REDACTED} stopped entirely, form twisted between two
directions.
"...stupid... making me... feel bad..."
The words barely reached ***, more emotion than sound. For a moment, *** thought it
might return.
Then {REDACTED} forced itself forward, movements lacking their usual grace. Time
seemed to stutter around its form—little moments rewinding as if it kept trying to turn back,
only to force itself forward again.
A speck. A memory. Nothing.
***'s eye lingered on the void, patient and empty. Waiting without understanding what it
waited for.
Seconds bled into minutes—but wait, hadn't more time passed? The moments felt strange,
compressed and stretched, as if {REDACTED}'s absence had broken something in time'sflow.
Minutes stretched into hours—or was it days? Without {REDACTED}'s presence,
chronology lost meaning.
Hours melted into days—surely years by now. The distinction blurred.
Days dissolved into years—or perhaps just heartbeats. Who could tell?
Years became ages.
But {REDACTED} did not return.
Just like mom and dad.
And so, now truly alone, *** closed its eye and drifted toward the only action left.
Sleep.
 
 
A gentle touch broke through the darkness, a faint yet persistent sensation that nudged
against ***'s awareness. Slowly, like a flower opening to the sun, *** stirred, her thoughts
gathering, coalescing into a fragile sense of self.
For a moment—just a moment—she expected to see starlight. Expected to feel the cold press
of void against her not-skin. Expected to hear—
"Little oooonee…wakey, wakey~"
The voice was wrong. No, not wrong—different. Like a familiar melody played on an
unfamiliar instrument. Sweet and lilting, yes, but missing the otherworldly harmonics that
usually danced beneath the Elk's words.
A drowsy whimper escaped her lips—strange, having lips that could whimper, a throat that
could make sounds without willing them. Her eyes, still heavy with sleep, fluttered open. The
muscles around them moved in ways she hadn't commanded, pulling and stretching in
patterns her body seemed to know but her mind didn't.
The shadows retreated, revealing a world painted in dark wood paneling that gleamed with
oil polish.
The walls around her glistened softly in the muted glow of brass oil lamps. Two empty seats
ahead caught her gaze—covered in rich, deep colors. Burgundy like spilled wine in
storybooks. Dark green like painted forests. Navy like the night sky in fairy tale illustrations.
But these colors breathed, shifting with each flicker of lamplight, alive in ways her picture
books never showed.A long, earnest yawn escaped—no, invaded—her body. The strange hollow feeling in her
throat demanded stretching, unhinging her jaw wider than felt proper, air rushing in to fill
spaces she didn't know existed inside her. Her new body knew this dance even if she didn't.
And beneath it all came a soft, rhythmic metallic heartbeat—click-clack, click-clack, click-
clack—while the floor swayed gently, like being rocked in a giant's careful palm. The whole
world seemed to breathe around her, walls creaking softly, a great mechanical lung
somewhere ahead releasing steam in satisfied sighs: chuff-chuff-chuff.
Something soft brushed against her hair. Lowering her eyes—and oh, how strange to have
eyes that needed lowering, a neck that bent—she saw her cheek resting against a pair of legs,
covered in black stockings.
"Fufufu, you're finally awake, little one~"
That almost-familiar voice again. *** scrunched her face, trying to catch the missing notes,
the absent echoes that should shimmer beneath the words.
She tilted her face upward, neck muscles moving in ways that felt both natural and alien. A
young woman looked down at her—delicate, refined features, like a princess from a fairy
tale. She wore a black Victorian-style outfit, high collar pressed neat, lace detailing around
the cuffs like frozen spiderwebs. Her dark hair flowed over her shoulders, framing a warm,
amused expression.
The Kind Elk.
Her human form, to be precise.
Oh. That's why the voice sounded different.
"Rise and shine~" She cooed, her voice soft as silk, familiar now that *** understood. "Did
you enjoy your little nap?"
*** She nodded groggily, sleep still tugging at her thoughts. She lifted her head from the elk's
lap, her small hands instinctively reaching up to rub her eyes—
"Ow!"
She'd pressed too hard, these new types of perceptors were more delicate than she'd expected.
Sparks of light danced behind her eyelids. Yhounden's hand gently caught hers, guiding them
to rub more softly, and *** found herself copying the motion exactly, storing it away. This is
how humans comfort their eyes.
But before she could fully take in her surroundings, a loud, piercing whistle shrieked through
the space—'WHEEE-OOOOO!'—like a metal beast announcing its presence. The walls
shuddered, the floor trembled, and ***'s new heart—she had a heart now, beating-beating-
beating so fast!—leaped like a frightened rabbit in her chest.
She instinctively burrowed closer to Yhounden, clutching the soft fabric of her dress, her
small hands trembling. The velvet felt different than storybook descriptions—not just "soft"but textured, with tiny ridges her fingers could trace.
"Oh?" A soft chuckle escaped Yhounden's lips. She carefully swept a few locks of black hair
from ***'s forehead, tracing soothing patterns on her back. *** unconsciously began
breathing in time with those patterns—in with the upstroke, out with the down. "Hush, hush~
Do not fret... That was just the whistle of the train."
***'s eyes blinked open slowly, confusion replacing her initial fear. She noticed how
Yhounden's hand moved and tried to memorize it—this is how mothers soothe their
daughters.
"...Train?"
The Elk smiled. "A train is like a giant, fast snake..." She drew out her words, tracing an
elongated shape in the air with her finger. "A snake that's friendly and gentle, unlike its kin.
Humans climb inside its big, comfy belly, and it carries them where they need to go."
***'s eyes widened with awe. In her mind bloomed an image of a massive green serpent with
a kind smile, its belly full of velvet seats. And suddenly the sounds made sense—the click-
clack was its scales against the earth, the chuff-chuff its breathing, the whistle its friendly
greeting!
"This train thingy sounds super cool!" she exclaimed, bouncing slightly in her seat. The
motion felt strange—her body going up while the train swayed side to side, creating a dizzy
dance of movement.
“Where is it?? I want to see it!"
Yhounden chuckled. "No need to look around, little one... we're already inside one~"
*** gasped, her gaze darting around the cabin in fresh awe. "Really??" She took in the dark
wood paneling, the cushioned seats, the brass fixtures that gleamed like the snake's internal
organs.
"So this is the train's belly..."
She murmured in wonder. It didn't look as she'd imagined—not red and fleshy like the whale
that swallowed Pinocchio, but polished and warm, with the sweet scent of coal smoke and
leather mixing with lavender from Yhounden's perfume.
Her attention was quickly drawn to a large window beside them. Rain streaks traced lazy
patterns on the glass.
Eagerly, she pressed her face close to it, her breath fogging the cold surface.
That's when she saw her—a pale little human girl with dark hair looking back, translucent
against the world beyond. For a heartbeat, *** didn't recognize the human face as her own.
Then the landscape stole her attention entirely."Ooooh..." Her breath fogged more glass as her eyes drank in the sight of rolling green hills
dotted with white sheep like scattered clouds. Farmhouses and cottages speckled the
landscape, smoke rising from their chimneys in greeting to their traveling snake.
So much green. But not the flat, still green of her picture books—this green breathed and
rippled, each blade of grass distinct, bending and swaying like tiny tentacles in the wind. The
yellow of the wheat fields wasn't just yellow but a thousand different yellows, shifting from
gold to amber to cream as clouds passed overhead.
And it was all moving. Moving while she sat still.
*** pressed harder against the glass, trying to understand. In the void, if something moved
away from you, you had to move to follow it. But here she was perfectly still—she could feel
the seat beneath her—yet the world slid past like pages turning in a book.
"But I'm not moving," she whispered, wonder and confusion tangling in her voice. "How is
the world moving if I'm not?"
"Do you like what you see?" Yhounden's voice interrupted her thoughts, gentle and warm.
She leaned closer, her breath adding to the fog on the window. *** unconsciously leaned the
same way, their heads tilting at identical angles.
"This is what humans call the countryside—a land far from the towns and cities."
"Cities…" *** repeated, the word foreign on her tongue, rolling it around like a strange
sweet. "What's a—"
But before she could finish, the door to their cabin slid open with a soft click—such a small
sound, yet it thundered in her new ears, making her whole body jolt.
"Good day to you, m'ladies. Might I trouble you for but a moment?"
A voice. Deep and rumbling, nothing like Yhounden's silk-soft tones. It vibrated through
***'s chest in a way that made her clutch at her dress, wondering if human bodies always felt
sounds so... physically.
"Tickets, if you'd be so kind."
***'s gaze snapped toward the door, her little heart—thump-thump-thumping so fast she
worried it might escape—leaping as she took in the figure standing there.
A human. A real human. Not flat like the Grimes and the princess in her books, but
dimensional, taking up space in ways she'd never imagined.
He had broad shoulders that filled the doorway, a sharp jaw shadowed with stubble —the
books never showed stubble— and neat sideburns that framed his face like dark parentheses.
His crisp, dark navy-blue jacket bore brass buttons that caught the lamplight—not painted
highlights but real reflections that moved when he breathed. His peaked cap sat at a precise
angle, its brass insignia winking like a tiny sun.And the smell of him—tobacco smoke clinging to wool, pomade keeping his hair neat, the
faint metallic scent of the brass polish on his buttons. The books never mentioned how
humans smelled.
His posture was firm and steady, his gaze attentive as he looked at them with polite
seriousness. Not the worshipful adoration of fairy tale princes, but something more...
Professional. Human.
Yhounden regarded the man with a calm, unsettling composure. "…Why yes, of course…"
she replied, much to ***'s surprise, using the language of the humans. The words flowed
from her throat so easily, not like when *** tried to speak. Her right hand moved gracefully
away from *** as she reached for a small purse at her side—black velvet with silver clasps
that clicked open like tiny teeth. "Just a moment…"
She pulled out two small, thin slips of paper, each marked with numbers and intricate
designs. ***'s eyes widened. Magic papers? They looked so fragile, yet apparently these
flimsy things granted passage through the train-snake's belly.
As Yhounden handed them to the inspector, *** found herself captivated by every detail of
the man. The way his gloved fingers—leather creaking softly—took the tickets. How his eyes
moved as he read, little muscles twitching beneath the skin. The slight shift of weight from
foot to foot that made his keys jangle at his belt.
So this is what a real human moves…
Her eyes studied him intently, cataloguing each difference from her fairy tales. He was
rougher somehow—lines around his eyes, a small scar on his knuckle, whiskers that looked
sharp rather than soft. Not like the smooth-faced knights and princes at all, but just as—no,
more fascinating.
Her lips parted ever so slightly. Questions bubbled up from somewhere deep, desperate to
escape. She wanted to know everything. Did all humans smell like tobacco and brass? Why
did they need papers to travel? How did his throat make such deep sounds?
"What's—""ФibЁ}╚—"
Yhounden's hand moved faster than thought, pressing over ***'s mouth—gentle but firm,
warm fingers sealing in the inhuman sounds before they could fully manifest. *** felt the
softness of Yhounden's palm, tasted the faint lavender of her perfume, noticed how perfectly
the hand shaped itself to silence her.
At the same moment, the inspector's face contorted. His free hand shot to his temple, pressing
hard as if trying to hold his skull together. His eyes squeezed shut, and for just a second, ***
saw real pain flicker across his features.
"Beggin' your pardon, miss," he managed, his voice strained as he forced his eyes open,
painting on an apologetic smile that didn't quite reach his eyes. "Don't know what's gotten on
me..."With slightly trembling fingers, he pulled out a strange tool—metal with a wooden handle—
and punched perfect round holes in the edges of the tickets. Chk-chk. The sound was
satisfying, final. A ritual completed. He returned them to Yhounden with a respectful nod,
though *** noticed he avoided looking directly at her now.
"Now then, enjoy the rest o' your journey."
With a slight bow—quick, eager to leave—he exited, sliding the door closed with a soft click
that seemed louder than before. His footsteps hurried down the corridor, accompanied by the
urgent jangle of his keys.
The moment he was gone, something hot and unfamiliar surged through ***'s chest. Her
cheeks burned—actually burned, like tiny fires under her skin. Her hands, without her telling
them to, clenched into fists so tight her new fingernails bit into her palms.
"WHY DID YOU DO THAT??!"
The words exploded out, louder than she'd ever spoken before. This feeling—this burning,
churning, tightness in her chest—Was this how the human body dealt with anger? It made her
want to hit things, to scream, to make Yhounden understand how unfair it all was.
"I…he…he's gone now!" She huffed—a sharp exhale through her nose that she didn't know
human bodies could make. Her arms crossed over her chest, and she turned her gaze away,
lower lip pushing out in a pout she must have seen in a storybook somewhere.
But the moment the words left her mouth, something cold and heavy dropped into her
stomach.
I shouldn't have—
The anger was already curdling into something else, something that made her want to shrink
into herself.
Yhounden remained silent for a heartbeat, and when *** glanced back, she caught something
flicker in the Elk's eyes. Surprise, yes, but underneath... was that satisfaction? The expression
vanished too quickly to be sure.
"Oh, little one…big sis is terribly, terribly sorry." Her voice held no anger, no hurt—only
gentle concern that made the guilt in ***'s stomach twist tighter. She should be mad. *** had
yelled at her. But instead, Yhounden's voice dripped honey-sweet worry. "I didn't mean to
make you angry…"
With a soft smile, Yhounden's fingers found ***'s hair, beginning their soothing dance. First
at the crown—gentle circles that made ***'s scalp tingle. Then down to the nape of her neck,
where tension she didn't know she was holding began to melt.
"Can you find in your heart to forgive big sis? Pretty, pretty please?"
*** kept her pout, but it was already wavering. The guilt made it hard to hold onto the anger
—like trying to grasp water. Each stroke of Yhounden's fingers seemed to pull the anger outof her, drawing it up and away like pulling threads from fabric. The burning in her cheeks
cooled. Her fists unclenched. The tight knot in her chest began to loosen.
This is how daughters forgive mothers, some part of her noted, filing the information away.
When mothers pet your hair just so, daughters stop being angry.
"I…I just wanted to know more about humans…." The words came out small and apologetic
now, nothing like her earlier shout. She pressed closer to Yhounden's hand, seeking both
comfort and forgiveness. "I thought that human could tell me…"
Slowly, reluctantly—making sure Yhounden saw the effort it took—she turned her gaze
back. Her expression still held disappointment like a child clutching a favorite toy, though the
anger had dissolved like sugar in rain.
"…So why did you stop me?"
Yhounden's fingers paused, just for a moment. When *** looked up, she saw that thoughtful
expression again—the one that made her look like a teacher about to impart an important
lesson.
"It's considered….rude for a child to speak to an adult without permission. If you want to
speak, you must wait for them to say their name first."
"Oh…" The explanation slotted into ***'s mind like a puzzle piece. Another human rule to
remember. She glanced down at herself—at her small hands, her child's dress, her little feet
that barely touched the floor.
So… I can't talk to humans unless they speak to me first…
A sigh slipped from her—long and dramatic, the kind she'd seen illustrated in her fairy tales
when princesses were especially put upon.
Being a human child is so limiting.
Yhounden chuckled, the sound warm enough to chase away the last wisps of guilt still
clinging to ***'s chest. "You were asking about cities, weren't you?" She nodded toward the
window. "Well... here it is."
***'s eyes sparked back to life. Eager to be good, to make up for her outburst, she pressed her
face to the glass with renewed enthusiasm—so hard her nose squished flat and her breath
fogged everything.
Gone were the gentle hills and pastures, replaced by... by...
*** blinked, her excitement stuttering.
This wasn't right.
Where were the crystal spires? The golden gates? In every story, cities had marble palaces
and streets that sparkled in the sun. But this...Sprawling streets twisted beneath a sky that wasn't blue or grey but something in-between,
heavy with... what was that white-grey stuff? It wasn't clouds—clouds were fluffy and
friendly. This hung low and thick, pouring from tall black fingers that pointed accusingly at
the sky. Dark buildings huddled together like frightened animals, their rooftops a maze of
angles and shadows.
"This city thingy looks... dirty..."
Dirty was mud on a princess's dress, or dust on a knight's armor. But this was different. This
was... she didn't have words for it. Her fairy tales had never shown her this.
Is this what cities really look like?
Her hands pressed harder against the glass, as if she could push through to understand better.
The white stuff—smoke, maybe?—moved differently than fog in stories. It didn't dance or
swirl prettily. It just... hung there. Choking the sky.
Still, even if it was dirty and strange, it was real. And real meant she could touch it, explore
it, understand why the fairy tales had lied—no, not lied, why they would lie? Why they'd left
this part out.
"Can we go there? Please? Please????"
The words tumbled out faster than her thoughts, her human body taking over. She bounced
on her seat—when had she learned to bounce?—making the springs creak. Her hands left
small prints on the window as she pulled back to look at Yhounden, then immediately
pressed close again, unable to stop moving.
Yhounden's lips curved into a soft smile, and she patted ***'s head. "No need to ask, little
one. You'll get to see it up close very soon."
Just then, footsteps echoed outside their compartment—measured, official—followed by a
voice different from their earlier visitor, rougher around the edges: "Ladies and gentlemen,
we'll be arriving at King's Cross Station shortly. Please prepare your belongings."
The click-clack beneath them began to slow, the rhythm growing lazy. *** felt it in her
stomach first—a strange pulling sensation, like invisible hands trying to push her forward
while the seat held her back. The train-snake was getting tired, ready to rest.
A low rumble built around them, different from before. Deeper. The carriages swayed in a
new pattern, and *** had to grab the window frame to steady herself. Her body wanted to
keep moving even as everything else slowed. Was this normal? Did humans feel this pulling-
pushing every time?
***'s eyes widened as she clutched Yhounden's sleeve with her free hand, her fingers
twisting the fabric. Every part of her wanted to move—feet kicking against the seat,
shoulders wiggling, that strange human energy that demanded release.
We're almost there!The city grew larger in the window, smoke and stone rushing up to meet them. She could see
people now—tiny figures scurrying along the streets like ants in her garden books. But these
ants wore dark colors, not the bright reds and blues of storybook crowds.
The train's breathing changed. Great gasps and sighs as the click-clack became click... clack...
click... The forward pull grew stronger, and *** found herself leaning back involuntarily, her
small body pressed into the seat.
Then came the hiss—long and satisfied, like the snake had finally found its resting place.
Steam swept past their window in great white billows, and for a moment the dirty city
vanished behind a veil of clean white. When it cleared, they were still.
The sudden absence of motion felt wrong. ***'s body kept expecting the sway, the click-
clack lullaby. Instead, new sounds rushed in—muffled voices, sharp whistles, the clatter of
boots on stone, metal grinding against metal. So many sounds, nothing like the simple
morning songs of fairy tale birds.
Yhounden rose with quiet elegance, not a hair out of place despite their journey. She
extended her hand to ***, who clasped it firmly—perhaps too firmly, her excitement making
her squeeze tighter than intended.
As *** stood, her legs wobbled slightly. They'd grown used to the train's motion and didn't
quite understand being still. She pressed closer to Yhounden, using the Elk's stability to
anchor herself in this world that was already proving stranger than any story had prepared her
for.
The fairy tales never mentioned the smoke, she thought, peering out one last time at the city
beyond the glass. Or the dirt. Or the way everything seems so... grey.
But then again, the fairy tales had never mentioned a lot of things.
 
 
Stepping off the train proved harder than expected. The gap between train and platform
yawned like a tiny canyon, and ***'s legs—still not quite obedient—wobbled as she tried to
judge the distance. Yhounden's hand steadied her as she made the small leap, her Mary Janes
clicking against stone in a way that sent vibrations up through her bones.
Then the world hit her all at once.
The mingling scents of iron, coal smoke, damp stone, and something else—hundreds of
human bodies in one space, each carrying their own mixture of perfume, sweat, pomade, and
wool—crashed into her senses like a wave. Her nose rebelled immediately.
"Bleaugh—ACHOO!"The sneeze exploded out of her, making her whole body jerk. She quickly covered her nose,
eyes watering, but it was like trying to hold back an ocean with her hands. The smells kept
coming—hot oil from the engines, wet dog from someone's pet, rotting vegetables from a
dropped crate, flowers from a lady's corsage.
Through her blurring vision, she tried to take in her surroundings, but the platforms were a
maze of motion. Ladies swept past in flowing skirts that billowed like storm clouds, their
parasols nearly catching her in the eye. Gentlemen in tailored coats moved with purpose,
their walking sticks clicking rhythms she couldn't follow. Workers in rough-spun clothing
hauled luggage and crates, grunting under the weight.
And the sounds—oh, the sounds! Voices layered upon voices, not the clear dialogue of
storybook conversations but a crushing wall of noise. Whistles shrieked. Metal clanged
against metal. Boots thundered on stone. Steam hissed from a dozen directions. The iron and
glass ceiling caught every sound and threw it back down, doubling, tripling the chaos.
***'s eyes darted frantically, trying to capture every detail, but it was like trying to read a
hundred books at once. A porter rushed past so close she felt the wind of his passing. A lady's
carpetbag swung near her head. Every few seconds, someone new brushed against her—
casual touches that sent sparks of alarm through her body. In the void, nothing touched her
without her permission. Here, contact came from everywhere.
So much noise!
So much movement!
So many people touching without asking!
"Wow…" she whispered, though the station's roar swallowed her voice completely.
Her gaze swept upward, desperate for something stable, and found the iron beams arching
overhead. They curved like... like the ribs of some great beast that had swallowed them all.
Was this what Jonah felt inside the whale? But no, this whale was made of metal and glass,
breathing steam instead of air.
Her eyes dropped to the polished marble floors—so different from dirt paths in forests or
castle stones. These reflected the gaslight in ways that made her dizzy. Everything here
bounced back—sound, light, even the heat from all these bodies.
A man bumped her shoulder in passing, and suddenly it was too much. *** clutched
Yhounden's hand with both of hers, ducking behind the Elk's skirts. After a thousand years of
silence, all this life was thrilling, yes, but also... frightening? Was that the right word? It made
her want to hide and explore at the same time.
Yhounden glanced down at her, smiling reassuringly as she gave ***'s hand a firm squeeze.
"Come now, little one. Let's find our way out of this chaos."
Guiding her toward the station's grand marble staircase, Yhounden moved with practiced

Empty wall. Just red brick and peeling posters, as if he'd never existed at all.
A gentle tug on her hand pulled her back to the present. She turned to find Yhounden

"Something wrong?"
*** blinked, glancing once more at the empty wall. Had he been real? Or was her mind

*** nodded slightly, though unease clung to her like the city's soot.
As they continued down the crowded street, she kept glancing over her shoulder, expecting to

Their journey led them next to Hyde Park, where the bustling noise and frenetic energy of
Camden softened, melting into a mist-laden silence. Here, the city's heartbeat seemed to
slow; fog drifted lightly across wide green fields and twisted pathways, and the dark waters

This was more like her storybooks—green spaces, water, mist that could hide magic. People

This is me now, she thought with satisfaction. Pretty. Human. Real.

Small.
Human.
Cute.

Human fingers. Human movements. Human child.

She pushed the doubt away. Of course she had. She was here, wasn't she? Walking in

Her hand was empty.

There—a few meters away, standing apart from the path, her dark figure silhouetted against

What kind of story is this human reading?
It doesn’t have a beginning nor a middle….

And why had the kind Elk sounded so angry about it?
Grown-ups are strange…
As they left Hyde Park, sounds crept in—distant vendor calls, the clip-clop of horses. Then

The crowds thickened again around Piccadilly Circus, but now *** found them less

That's when she saw them.

The big bad wolf. After he ate grandmother.

All the better to grab you with.

strange relief as she took in the girl's fierce, commanding presence. This girl had appeared

"...Malýshka?"
Soft but clear, tinged with an unfamiliar rhythm, a voice slipped into ***'s ears like water
flowing over jagged shards of glass. It was followed by a hand waving slowly in front her
face, back and forth.
***'s gaze remained fixed on the blonde girl, but her eyes held that peculiar emptiness of
someone whose mind had drifted far away. She could see the pale face, the moving lips, but it
was as if she were looking through glass—seeing without truly perceiving. Her thoughts still
circled around red fabric, how it would frame those blonde strands so perfectly...
"Malýshka!"
The voice became insistent, and the hand retracted slightly. The thumb and finger pressed
together, the latter suddenly snapping downward to strike the palm with a sharp click.
The sound startled ***, snapping her out of her thoughts. Her gaze suddenly focused, truly
seeing the figure before her for the first time.
A young human girl, knelt at eye level. Her blue eyes—like mirrors of water on the verge of
freezing—locked intently on ***'s.
"Are you okay?"
She tilted her head to the side, loose blonde strands brushing her pale cheeks as they fell. She
looked almost like a lone wolf sizing up its prey, gauging whether it was worth the effort of
the hunt.

curious. Expectant.
Waiting for something.
An answer.

bearing faint handprints where the man had gripped—as Yhounden's warning echoed in her
mind.

each other, fingers twisting and pulling in anxious patterns.

What do I do?!

man sprawled a few paces behind the human girl—none held the answers she needed.

the earlier throngs had thinned, the square still pulsed with activity.
Women in bonnets exchanged hushed gossip near the fountains, their hands clutching
parasols or wicker baskets brimming with fresh goods.
Nearby, a dapper gentleman gestured animatedly at a street vendor, his tailored coat flaring
slightly as he haggled over the price of a hand-carved walking cane.
The rhythmic clatter of horses' hooves echoed as carriages rolled past, their occupants either
peering out from behind curtained windows or lost in their own reveries.
***'s wide eyes darted around, absorbing the mesmerizing chaos. She held tightly to the
blonde girl's corset, her small hand clutching the fabric like a lifeline. The world seemed

Each step brought a small, annoying ache to her feet—the Mary Janes that had seemed so
pretty this morning now pinched with every stride. Not truly painful, but a constant reminder
of how long they'd been walking.
Every now and then, the blonde girl would stop a passerby—a merchant adjusting his cart, a
nanny herding a well-dressed teenage boy, or a laborer wiping sweat from his brow—and
show them the drawing of Yhounden.

to admire the portrait, marveling at its detail.

his round spectacles. "Are you the artist, young lady?"

The night had fully enveloped London, shrouding the massive stone arches of another bridge
in darkness that flickered with the pale glow of gas lamps. Their faint, steady light did little
to banish the oppressive gloom.
The Thames below murmured faintly, its black waters churning sluggishly against the
bridge's pilings. The air carried the rank, fetid scent of the river—brine, decay, and the
industrial grime that clung like a second skin.
Silhouettes lingered along the edges of the bridge, their shadows stretching long and warped
under the gaslight. They were mostly men—bulky, sturdy, seasoned from years working the
docks. Coarse fabrics and patched trousers their only dignity now.
"♪ …Pride and greed, lust and fame….♪"
"♪….Twisting men since time arcane….♪"
One leaned heavily against a stone balustrade, his knuckles bone-white as he gripped it
tightly. His eyes darted about, wild and unfocused, and his lips moved in frantic, soundless
prayer.
Another stood nearby, his face pale and drenched in sweat, clutching a bottle with trembling
hands.
"♪...Bound their kin to endless wail...♪"
"♪...Bringing madness, bringing pain...♪"
A third paced aimlessly, his boots scraping against the stone, muttering under his breath like a
man locked in conversation with ghosts.One more sat slumped against the base of a lamp post, his knees drawn to his chest. His
hands clutched at his head as he rocked back and forth, muttering a fevered refrain:
"Please stop… please stop…"
Yet amidst this tableau of despair, a slender, graceful figure made her way along the bridge
with light, almost dance-like steps.
"♪...For mere desires they always fight...♪"
She balanced along the narrow edge of the balustrade as though it were the finest tightrope,
her arms outstretched for balance. Her dark dress flowed with her movements, its fabric
shimmering faintly under the gaslight. The lace collar framed her pale face—a face almost
too perfect to be human.
In one hand, she clutched a small velvet satchel, its contents shifting with soft clicking
sounds as she moved.
Her hair, an endless cascade of black silk, caught the flickering lamplight as she swayed.
Each step was accompanied by a whimsical hum as she sang to herself, her voice cutting
through the gloom with childlike glee.
"♪...Through clinking coins, through kingdoms' might....♪"
One step, two steps, then a little jump. She moved with the careless grace of a child, pausing
occasionally to sway her hips or spin lightly on her toes as though performing for an unseen
audience.
"♪...And as wealth and power drown their insight...♪"
"♪...Their black souls, to behold, will be a delight!♪"
The final notes drifted into silence, her head tilting back in a mock show of triumph, eyes
closing as if savoring the torment etched into the air around her.
"Ufufufu~♪ That was a good one~ ♡ "
She muttered in satisfaction before slowly opening her eyes, their black irises twinkling with
amusement as she caught sight of one of the despairing men sprawled on the cobblestones in
a fetal curl, crying and whimpering like a newborn infant.
"Why are you crying, my friend?" She asked with almost maternal concern before crouching
in front of him. Her hands reached for his rough cheeks, rubbing them gently.
"Please…leave me alone…" He simply whimpered, curling further into himself, a gesture
that only boosted her amusement as a soft giggle escaped her lips.
"Leave you alone?" She repeated in playful surprise. "But why should I? After all, weren't
you and your friends saying how much you all were eager to 'play' with me…?"Her hand moved from his cheek to his nose, booping its tip lightly again and again, a gesture
so absurdly carefree it only deepened his misery.
"And how I was gonna 'sing' for you lots and lots….and so I did~♪" She cooed, and with each
boop her palm slowly took the semblance of a pale hoof.
“But then all of you started acting so funny...crying…screaming….was my voice out of tune,
perhaps?"
The man couldn't take it anymore. With a guttural cry, he lurched to his feet and staggered

The night erupted.
A sound ripped through the air—a single, catastrophic burst that hit London like an invisible
cannon blast. It wasn't quite a scream, wasn't quite words, but something that existed in the
space between.
The effect was instantaneous.
Men and women who until now had been walking or carrying out their duties dropped to
their knees, hands flying to their ears as they let out guttural groans. Some clawed at their
temples, their faces contorted in anguish. Others simply collapsed where they stood,
consciousness fleeing rather than confronting the impossible sound.
"What in Heaven's name was that?!""Did you hear it?! Sounded like the devil himself!"
From further down the street, the frenzied whinny of horses rang out, high-pitched and
panicked. Hooves clattered violently against the cobblestones, the sounds interwoven with
sickening cracks of wood splintering and bones crashed.
"The horses! They've gone mad!"
Yog and Yhounden stood untouched amidst the devastation, two still figures in a sea of
writhing bodies. The satchel in Yhounden's grip suddenly yanked upward as if caught in a
violent gust, the velvet bag flapping and straining against her hold like a flag in a hurricane.
"Oh my~" Yhounden murmured, raising her free hand to her lips in mock surprise. "Well, this
is awkward... Sorry, I guess...?"
Yoggy's glare could have frozen fire, but he said nothing. Instead, he turned sharply on his
heel, nostrils flaring as a new scent reached them—the metallic tang of blood carried on the
wind from Southwark's direction.
Without a word, he began striding toward the source of the chaos.
"Wait for me, darling~" Yhounden sang, trailing just behind him. Her dark dress flowed like
liquid shadow, her steps deliberate yet unnaturally smooth, as though the chaos around her
was nothing but a stage for her amusement.
As they neared the street where the disturbance had erupted, the evidence of destruction grew
clearer. An overturned carriage lay crushed against a building's facade, its wheels still
spinning lazily in the air. Glass shards glittered across the cobblestones like deadly stars,
mixing with splintered wood and darker stains that reflected wetly in the gaslight.
The murmur of the crowd—panicked, confused, terrified—faded, consumed by something
more primal echoing in the night air.
A cry.
Desperate.
Piercing.
The unmistakable, heart-wrenching grievance of a little girl.

The muscles under her cheek went rigid. Then, slowly, surrendered. A sigh escaped Pa-lach's
lips, long and shaky as autumn leaves.
Silence stretched. Just their breathing—***'s steady, Pa-lach's labored. From the main street
came distant sounds: a carriage clattering over stones, a drunk man singing off-key, a cat's
territorial yowl. London's night symphony, muffled by brick and shadow.
"…You really… want to watch me… sleep that badly… hmm?"
*** lifted her head just enough to nod vigorously against the shoulder, feeling the rough wool
of Pa-lach's coat scratch her cheek.
Another sigh. Defeat wrapped in exhaustion.
"…Stubborn… little brat…" Eyes fluttering. Fighting to stay open. Losing. "…Fine. Only…
only for a few… minutes… Understood?"
*** nodded again, smaller this time.
"Just… rest my eyes… you better not… pull any… prank… while I'm… out…"
With that final warning, the blonde’s eyes surrendered. Her head tilted sideways, finding the
brick wall like a cold, unforgiving pillow. The muscles under ***'s cheek slowly, slowly
unknotted.
Finally… she's resting now…
Good.
For the first time since meeting this strange human girl, she looked…
Defenseless.
Fragile even. Like—
Like a princess.
No.
Comparing her to those spoiled, useless things was an insult. Pa-lach was stronger. Braver.
She'd jumped through windows, faced down metal wands, taken thunder into her body and
kept walking.
Better.
Only her, out of them all, deserved a happily ever after.*** shifted on the unforgiving stones, pressing her small shoulder more firmly against Pa-
lach's warmth. Where their bodies touched, heat leaked through fabric—precious degrees in
the October chill. The contact grounded her. Made her real in ways the void never had.
The waiting game began.
Time moved strangely here. Not like in the void where it didn't exist, but thick and slow like
honey. She tried to track it by sounds: footsteps passing the alley mouth (seven sets). Church
bells in the distance (once). Her own heartbeat (she lost count at two hundred).
The cobblestones grew colder, their dampness seeping through her dress like seeking fingers.
Her human body responded with shivers she couldn't quite control. Strange how cold felt so
different with nerves and skin. In the void, cold was just absence. Here it had teeth.
Yet this cold felt… bearable. The soft rhythm of Pa-lach's breathing—in, out, in, out—
created its own warmth. A living metronome against the silence. And where their shoulders
pressed together, heat pooled and held, creating a small island of almost-rightness in the chill.
Silence and solitude are much more bearable with someone to share them with…
She kept herself occupied with her still-novel flesh. Rolled her thumbs in endless circles,
hypnotized by the joint's smooth rotation. Discovered she could pop her knuckles—such a
satisfying crack! Traced the lines on her palms like reading a map to nowhere.
Her feet performed their own ballet: flex, point, flex, point. The leather of her Mary Janes
creaked softly. She found that if she pressed her toes just right, she could make them crack
too. So many hidden sounds in a human body. Like a house settling, but portable.
Every now and then, Pa-lach would shift. Tiny movements—fingers twitching like they
sought invisible daggers. Head turning against brick, seeking comfort that wasn't there.
Sometimes her breathing would catch, hiccup, resume at a different tempo.
*** frowned at these signs but pushed worry away. Pa-lach was strong. The strongest. She
just needed rest after falling from high places and fighting bad men and walking while
leaking.
Just rest.
Yet, as the minutes stretched into what felt like hours, marked only by shadows crawling like
dying things across the alley walls, something began to feel undeniably... off. Pa-lach's
breathing, previously soft but steady, had grown quieter. Shallower. Like a candle flame
guttering in still air.
*** leaned closer, straining to hear. There—the faintest whisper of air. But too faint. Far too
faint.
She turned fully toward the blonde, peering through the gloom. What she saw sent ice
flooding through her borrowed veins.Sweat beaded Pa-lach's forehead like morning dew, but wrong—this was October cold, not
summer heat. Her skin had gone past snow-white to something else. Translucent. Like she
could see through to the brick wall behind. Skin stretched over sharp bones like wet paper.
What's… what's happening…??
Are humans supposed to look like that when sleeping??
Then—worse. A bluish tinge around Pa-lach's lips. The color of drowned things. Of fairy tale
curses right before the princess stops breathing forever.
***'s gaze dropped to where Pa-lach's hand pressed her side. The dark stain she'd noticed
before—it was moving. Growing.
A crimson flower blooming in fast-forward. Red petals unfurling across dark fabric, each
new inch of spread like another petal opening. The center so dark it looked black, the edges
bright as fresh paint. It glistened. Breathed. Grew.
*** stared. Red. Where had she seen—
A picture from Yhounden's books. Prince triumphant, princess saved, and in the corner—the
dragon. The defeated monster. Still and broken with a great red pool spreading from where
the hero's sword struck true.
In stories, red spreading meant the monster was finished.
Defeated.
Dead.
Does that mean…?
Her hand moved without permission. Trembling fingers brushed Pa-lach's forehead. Cold.
Damp. Like touching a fish at market. Wrong temperature. Wrong texture.
Wrong wrong wrong.
She's dying.
No…
Her human heart skipped. Stuttered. Raced.
No…!
Breathing came fast and shallow, matching Pa-lach's but for different reasons.
No!
She was on her feet. When? How? Didn't matter.NO!
The alley mouth. The street. Gas lamps bleeding yellow circles on wet stones. Her feet
moved wrong—too fast for her brain, too slow for her panic. She stumbled. Caught herself.
Ran.
The city swallowed her whole.
Click-click-click went her Mary Janes, the sound sharp as breaking bones. Her lungs burned.
Human bodies weren't made for cosmic terror. They broke too easy, tired too fast. But she
pushed.
Harder. Faster.
A man ahead. Tall shadow by a tavern door, pipe smoke wreathing his head. Could he help?
But as she drew close, she saw his face in the lamplight—hard eyes, cruel mouth twist. Like
Jimmy's face right before the thunder-wand. Her feet veered left. Away. No.
Another figure. Female this time, shawl-wrapped. But her hands moved quick at her sides,
counting coins with the sharp movements of someone who'd knife you for tuppence. ***
ducked into an alley. No.
Each face glimpsed was wrong. Too sharp. Too hollow. Too much like monsters pretending
to be people. Where were the Josiahs? The ones with soft voices and gentle hands? Did they
only exist in daylight?
Stupid… weak… human body!
She gasped the thought, pressed against cold brick in another narrow alley. Her chest heaved.
Spots danced in her vision like dying stars.
Then—voices. Close. Familiar?
She turned her head.
Three small figures near overturned crates and bins. Recognition bloomed.
The children.
"Oi!"
The smallest nearly tumbled into a bin before his sister caught him.
"Pip, you little clot!" She scolded, pinching his ear. "How many times I gotta tell ya? Don't
go divin' headfirst into every bleedin' bin!"
She turned to the older boy. "Find anythin' decent over there, Billy?"
Billy shook his head. "Nah, Ang. It's all rotten through. Maggots thicker'n fleas. One bite o'
that'd be a one-way ticket straight to the bone orchard, it would.""Blast it all!" Ang threw a fist against brick. "Right then. Useless here. Let's keep movin'…
maybe closer to the docks…"
A small growl interrupted. Pip's stomach.
"Big s-sis… I'm hungry…" His voice broke ***'s heart in new ways. "Can we… can we
maybe eat today…?"
Ang knelt instantly, ruffling his matted hair. "Yes, Pip, my ducky. I swear on… on the Lord
Almighty hisself… this'll be the day. We'll finally get rid o' that pesky empty feelin' right
there."
She tapped his concave stomach. He smiled like she'd promised him kingdoms.
"Really? You promise?"
"Cross me heart." Though doubt flickered in her eyes.
"Blimey, sis, how many times've you promised that?" Billy's dry voice cut in. "Best not count
yer chickens before they hatch, eh? Or yer pies before they're nicked."
"Oi! Cheeky little sod! How dare you defy yer boss?"
She lunged playfully, catching Billy in a headlock, knuckles rubbing his scalp while Pip
giggled.
"Gerroff, Aggie! Leggo!"
"Not till I scrub that pesky disobedience right outta yer skull!"
*** watched from shadows. Her hand found her pocket. The pie. Still there. Still hers. These
children who called her monster, who'd kicked and bit—they knew these streets.
What if they know how to fix her?
What if they know someone who can stop the red flower?
Decision made, *** took a breath and stepped out of the deeper shadows, closing the gap
between herself and the trio.
All three heads snapped towards the soft sound of her footsteps instantly, their bodies tensing,
wary as stray cats sensing intrusion. Ang's face hardened immediately as she recognized ***
approaching them.
"You again…" she hissed, her voice laced with suspicion and a dismissive scoff. She
straightened up, putting herself between *** and her brothers. "Still skulkin' about, are we?
What is it this time? Have you come back just to show yerself off again, you little piece of—"
Her words died abruptly in her throat right as *** stopped just before them, and without a
word, she moved her arms forward, pressing her small palms together, the slightly squashed,greasy meat pie held carefully between them like a sacred offering.
The orphan girl stared at the offered pie, then up at ***'s face, her expression shifting rapidly
from hostility to unfiltered surprise, then cautious disbelief. Slowly, hesitantly, her own grimy
hand reached out, fingers brushing ***'s before carefully taking the pie.
"…Thanks…" Aggie mumbled, sounding genuinely bewildered. "…I guess."
Without another word to ***, she immediately turned and offered the pie to Pip. The little
boy's eyes shone bright as polished shillings as he realized what it was. With a happy squeak,
he snatched it away from his sister and began taking huge, ravenous bites, crumbs flying,
barely seeming to chew.
"Oi!" Billy protested beside them. "Couldn't ya split it in two? I'm starvin' too, y'know!"
"Shut yer gob, idiot." Ang snapped back, though without real heat, her eyes still watching Pip
devouring the pie. "We'll get somethin' for ourselves later. Let 'im 'ave this."
Just then, *** gently tapped Aggie on the arm. The orphan girl turned back around slowly,
impatience returning to her features.
"Now what?" she demanded.
*** held up her small notebook, already flipped open to a fresh page. Scrawled hastily in
dark ink were the words:
'I need help. My friend is hurt. Can you come with me?'
Ang squinted, leaning slightly forward to peer at the words in the dim light. She frowned.
"What the blazes is this?" she asked, looking back at ***. "Can't you speak normal? Lost yer
tongue?"
*** shook her head mutely, pushing the notebook a little closer towards Aggie's eyes. In her
mind, the crimson flower kept blooming. Spreading. Each second wasted was another petal
unfurling across Pa-lach's jacket.
The orphan girl let out a long groan of pure annoyance. "Oh, for crying out loud… Billy!"
she called over her shoulder. "Get your swotty arse over here and see what the hell this mute
little dolly wants now."
Billy shuffled closer, peered over Aggie's shoulder at the notebook, his brow furrowed in
concentration as he slowly deciphered the words. "…It seems…" he announced finally,
looking up at his sister, "…she wants our help. Says her friend is hurt."
Aggie snorted derisively. "Help? We ain't got time nor energy to be helpin' strangers,
especially fancy little mutes like her."
She crossed her scrawny arms, looking *** up and down skeptically. Then, perhaps seeing
the genuine desperation in her wide, pleading eyes, she paused.Her gaze drifted around the alley, calculating, until it snagged on something pasted to the
opposite wall—a brightly colored, slightly faded poster advertising a local baker, depicting a
truly enormous, multi-layered, sumptuously decorated birthday cake dripping with icing and
crowned with candied cherries.
"Alright, listen up, mute," she said, turning back to ***, her voice hardening again with
shrewdness. "We ain't runnin' errands for free. But… if you really want our help so badly…"
Her eyes flicked towards the poster, then back to ***.
"You see that picture? Get back to your fancy place and bring us a big cake. Just like that one.
Fresh. Then maybe… maybe we'll see about helpin' yer friend."
A…'cake'…??
*** followed Aggie's gaze to the poster. Her eyes widened slightly as she took in the image
of this so-called 'cake'. It looked… complicated. Layers upon layers. Where would she even
—
Get back to your fancy place? Bring us cake?
But she is dying NOW!
Her heart hammered. The red flower wouldn't wait for her to find some fancy place, convince
someone to make cake, bring it back. By then Pa-lach would be cold as the void, still as the
monster in the picture book, gone gone gone—
"Ooooh, cake…" Pip breathed beside them, his eyes, previously focused solely on the last
crumbs of pie, now wide and fixed dreamily on the poster, a thin line of drool escaping the
corner of his mouth.
"Yeah…" Billy added, his voice taking on a hushed, reverent tone as he stared at the image.
"A soft, yummy cake… all fluffy inside… with so much cream… proper cream, made from
the finest… milk…"
Milk.
The word cut through ***'s panic like lightning. The memory surfaced—cool glass in her
hands, smooth sweetness on her tongue. Josiah's gift. If milk tasted that good, then cake made
with it…
Wait.
My powers.
She'd made dolls from nothing. Changed herself into human shape. Why not cake? No
searching, no wasting time while Pa-lach's life leaked away. Just… make it. Now.
Taking a deep breath, *** closed her eyes tight, shutting out the grimy alley, the orphans'
curious stares.The world went dark behind her lids. She pictured the cake from the poster—the shape, the
height, the layers she imagined might hide inside, the thick white icing, the bright red
cherries crowning the top.
The air changed first. Temperature dropping like winter arriving between heartbeats. The
smell of garbage and coal smoke faded, replaced by ozone and starlight, if starlight had a
scent.
Under her feet, the cobblestones groaned. Stone flowing like thick syrup, rising and
flattening. She felt it through her thin shoes—the ground reshaping itself, becoming
something new. A pedestal. A plate.
Sound next. A hum too low to properly hear, felt more in bones than ears. Reality bending
made a noise like crystal singing, like the universe clearing its throat.
Her skin prickled. Every tiny hair standing on end as power flowed through her small frame.
It didn't hurt, exactly, but felt like being too full—like she'd swallowed too much milk and
now it wanted to get out.
The sensation built behind her sternum, spreading through her arms, pooling in her palms.
"B-Big sis… what… what's happenin'…?"
Pip's voice, thin with awe and fear.
"It's… impossible…"
Billy's whisper, closer to prayer than speech.
The hum crescendoed. The ozone smell sharpened. For one eternal moment, *** felt the
weight of creation pressing down, the terrible responsibility of making something from
nothing.
Then—release.
The power flowed out of her like exhaled breath. The air shimmered, twisted, solidified.
Slowly, *** opened her eyes.
Standing on the cobblestones between her and the orphans, where only empty air had been
moments before, was… a cake. Her cake. Rising from a perfectly smooth stone plate that
hadn't existed seconds ago, circular and flat as a serving dish.
Multi-layered, covered in thick white frosting, dotted with bright red spheres. Exactly like the
poster. Exactly as she'd imagined.
A triumphant, confident smile curved ***'s lips.
See? Just what you wanted. Easy!Now help me please!
She raised her face, beaming expectantly towards the human children.
The expressions she met, however, were the exact opposite of what she expected.
Pip let out a strangled whimper, scrambling backward so fast he tripped over his own feet.
His hands clawed at Ang's skirts as he pulled himself behind her legs, only his wide, white-
rimmed eyes visible.
Billy's face had gone the color of old cheese. His mouth worked soundlessly, opening and
closing like the fish at market. One trembling hand rose to make some sort of sign across his
chest—cross-shaped, desperate.
And Ang… Ang stood frozen like someone had turned her to stone. The color drained from
her face so completely *** could see blue veins beneath the skin. Her pupils had shrunk to
pinpricks.
"You…" The word came out cracked, barely audible. Ang's whole body began to shake—tiny
tremors that started in her hands and spread outward. "H-how did you… What are you?"
She took a stumbling step back, dragging Pip with her. Her heel caught on uneven
cobblestone. She nearly fell, caught herself against the alley wall. Left a sweaty handprint on
the brick.
Billy found his voice, though it came out three octaves higher than normal. "It… it can't
be…" His eyes darted between *** and the cake like he was watching for it to transform into
something worse.
"Is she… Is she a demon…? A witch?"
His fingers fumbled for something at his neck—a small wooden cross on a fraying string. He
clutched it so tight his knuckles went white.
*** blinked, her smile crumbling into confusion.
Demon? Witch? What are they talking about…?
Are they shy...?
She knelt down, taking hold of the stone plate. Already she could feel wrongness through her
palms—the cake's weight shifting, becoming uneven. She rose and walked forward, holding
her creation out towards them again, eager to complete the transaction.
But her advance only triggered pure panic.
"GET BACK!"
Ang threw one arm out to shield her brothers, the other hand making that same desperate
cross-sign. "Don't come any closer! Don't you dare!"Pip was sobbing now, face buried in Ang's skirts. Billy had pressed himself flat against the

"She made it from nothin'," Billy whispered, horrified realization dawning. "From bleedin'
nothin'. If she can do that… if she can make things appear…"
"She could make us disappear," Ang finished, her voice hollow with dread. "Could turn our
insides out. Make our hearts stop. Make us explode like Guy Fawkes dolls—"
"Get that thing away!" Ang's shriek cut through the night. "Get it away from me and my
brothers, you… you monster!"
The word hit *** like a physical blow. Again.
Monster.
Grabbing Billy's hand with one trembling fist and Pip's with the other, Ang turned and fled.
Their feet slapped against wet stones, Pip's sobs echoing off narrow walls. Billy kept looking
back over his shoulder, terrified *** might follow, might point at them and make them vanish
like she'd made the cake appear.
What…?
*** stood frozen, watching their silhouettes shrink into darkness. The plate grew heavier in
her hands. Or maybe that was just her arms, suddenly weak as overcooked noodles.
What...what did I do wrong…?
She looked down at her creation. Already failing. The perfect white frosting had begun to
sag, losing its sharp edges. Tiny rivulets of white ran down the sides like tears. The layers
beneath showed through, also softening. The red spheres on top rolled slightly, leaving trails
in the melting surface.
Like ice sculptures in summer. Like snow castles in rain. Beautiful until warmth found them.
They wanted a cake… and I gave them the cake…
Hesitantly, she dipped a finger into the collapsing frosting and brought it to her lips. Sweet.
Too sweet. Cold in the wrong way, like eating winter. The texture was... off. Not quite solid,
not quite liquid. Like it couldn't decide what it wanted to be. Like she'd tried to build
something from a memory of a memory—milk pretending to be cream pretending to be cake.
Not unpleasant exactly. Just... wrong.
With a soft clink, *** let the plate drop. It hit the cobblestones and cracked down the middle.
The cake slumped further, spreading across the broken stone like a white wound. In minutes
it would be puddle. In an hour, just a sticky memory.
She forced the confusion down. Pressed the hurt into a small, hard ball behind her ribs where
it couldn't touch her. The blonde girl needed help. That was what mattered. Not words. Notfear. Not the way Pip had sobbed into his sister's skirts.
I need help—if they won't, others will.
*** squared her small shoulders. Lifted her chin the way Pa-lach did when facing hard
things. The blonde's cool mask didn't quite fit her face, but she wore it anyway. Stepped past
her ruined creation—already it looked more like spilled milk than cake—and out of the alley.
The street beyond was a narrow canyon between leaning buildings. Darkness pooled in
doorways. Broken windows stared down like dead eyes. Her Mary Janes rang too loud on the
stones—click-clack-click-clack—the only sound besides distant thunder that might have been
carriage wheels or her own heart.
Walk. Just walk.
Find someone. Anyone.
But her mind kept circling back. The terror in their eyes. Real terror. Not the play-fear from
fairy tales where children scream but secretly delight. This was the fear reserved for real
monsters. The kind that made mothers bar doors. Made fathers sharpen knives. Made
children hide under beds and pray to gods *** didn't understand.
Monster.
Why?
One foot. Other foot. The rhythm helped.
Why run away in fear? I did what they asked.
Turn left at the corner. More darkness. More silence.
A bad, evil monster wouldn't have done that… right?
The thought came with images from Yhounden's books. Monsters hoarding gold. Monsters
eating children. Monsters laughing at suffering.
A bad, evil monster would have taken the meat pie and devoured it alone.
Past a broken street lamp. Glass crunched under her shoe.
Would have let them starve.
A cat yowled somewhere. *** flinched.
Would have laughed at their tears.
Would have—
"…And the Lord WEEPS!"The voice exploded through the night, making *** stumble. Loud. Powerful. Filled with

"Weeps, I tell you, for those who have lost their way!"
It was followed immediately by a low murmur of other voices—perhaps a dozen or more—

Curiosity, sharp and immediate, pierced through ***'s lingering sadness and confusion.
What was that?
Who was shouting?
Moving silently on her small Mary Janes, she crept down the alley—the source of the

A small crowd had gathered in what might generously be called a square—really just where

Perhaps twenty souls clustered there. Factory workers still dusted with cotton fibers, their
faces gaunt from sixteen-hour shifts. Washerwomen with hands raw from lye, babies tied to

They formed a loose semi-circle around a splintered wooden crate that had once held tea
from India, now repurposed as a pulpit.
"Who toil not for their salvation, but for THE MACHINES! THE FACTORIES! THE
GEARS THAT GRIND AND DEVOUR MEN'S SOULS AND SPIT OUT THEIR
BODIES!"
At the center of it all—the shouting human.
He was younger than his voice suggested, perhaps thirty winters, but worn like old leather.
His black robes had been mended so many times they resembled a patchwork of shadows.
The collar, once white, had yellowed like old teeth. His face was all angles—sharp
cheekbones, sharper nose, a jaw that could cut glass.
Brown hair hung lank to his shoulders, and his eyes... his eyes burned with the particular
madness of those who'd seen too much truth or too little food.
He flung an accusatory arm out, pointing dramatically at a weary-looking man trying to push
past the edge of the crowd.
"You there!"The accused froze mid-step.
"Yes, you! DO YOU KNOW WHERE YOU GO WHEN YOUR BODY FINALLY FAILS?
WHEN THE GEARS ARE DONE WITH YOU, WHEN THEY SPIT OUT YOUR
BROKEN BONES AND YOUR CHILDREN STARVE IN THE GUTTER?? DO YOU?!"
A sudden streak of red cut through the air. A tomato—overripe and dripping, arcing toward
the preacher like a comet of produce.
"Shut up, you mad bastard!" a gruff voice shouted from the anonymous darkness.
"Aye, go back to Bedlam where ya belong!" another voice added. A woman near the front
crossed herself and shushed them. Two young factory girls giggled nervously, then fell silent
when an older woman glared.
With surprising agility, the preacher ducked. The tomato sailed past and exploded against the

Pa-lach's jacket. He rose instantly back to his full height.
"Foolish idiots! Blinded by your own squalor!" he bellowed, somehow louder, feeding on

He gestured wildly, nearly losing balance on his makeshift pulpit. "Satan himself weeps! Not
tears of sorrow, mind you, but tears of LAUGHTER—for we've all strayed so far into the
muck and filth of this 'modern age', we no longer ken the stench of our own rot!"
*** tilted her head slightly, blinking in mild confusion.
This human… seems a tad bit too much...energetic.
Her gaze drifted away from the animated man, sweeping instead over the gathered crowd.
Men, women, a few older children. Some leaned forward, drinking in every word. Others
rolled their eyes and muttered about gin being a better sermon. A few just stood hollow-eyed,
perhaps seeking any distraction from empty bellies and emptier pockets.
And as her eyes scanned the faces, a familiar spark ignited within her, chasing away the last
dregs of despair. Hope. People. A whole group of them! With all these humans gathered,
surely... surely there must be someone kind among them?
Someone good?
Someone like… a Grimi?
Yes! A virtuous, kind-hearted Grimi, perhaps hidden amongst the common folk, just waiting
for a chance to perform a noble deed.
Leaving the relative cover of the alley wall, *** clasped her small hands together before her
chest and carefully, cautiously began to make her way toward the edge of the crowd."Aye! That's right!" The preacher's voice cracked above the murmur, pacing his crate like a
caged animal.
"So steeped are we in the Devil's comforts, so BLIND are we made by the smoke of progress
and the false promise of prosperity, that we can no longer distinguish the righteous from the
damned! The furnaces burn day and night, but 'tis not coal they consume—'TIS THE VERY
SOULS OF MEN!"
He paused, leaning forward with the practiced timing of one who'd given this performance

The crowd's energy shifted. Some scoffed—"Always demons with this one.”—but others

even NOW!"
His voice dropped to a stage whisper that somehow carried to every ear. "Hiding itself, it is!
Under the sweet coat of an innocent noble child, the better to lure naive souls onto the path of
PERDITION! To toy with their immortal essence in its perverse, unholy plays!"
A disturbed ripple passed through the assembly. “Noble children, my arse.” one snorted—but
fear bloomed on other faces. A dock worker's wife clutched the wooden cross at her throat.
Someone whispered a prayer. Another made the sign against evil.
The preacher took a brief, theatrical pause, letting the fear sink in, taking a deep breath to
continue his sermon. He cast a brief glance over the crowd, assessing their reaction, and it

near the edge of the gathering.
A petite little girl. Dressed in fine, if slightly dishevelled, clothes – a dark jacket over a white
dress. Standing out from the rougher attire of the locals like a single droplet of pure oil
shimmering amidst murky water., walking slowly, hesitantly, towards them.
His arm shot out, finger jabbing toward her.

Every head in the crowd snapped around instantly, dozens of pairs of eyes fixing directly

She froze mid-step, caught in the sudden, intense glare of collective attention like a small

Why… why are they looking at me like that?The murmur of the crowd shifted, sharpened, turning from unease to suspicion, then hostility.
“See how it matches the description given by those poor innocents!”
the preacher continued, his voice rising with righteous indignation, feeding the crowd’s

“See the fine clothes, attempting to mimic purity! Oh, these detestable creatures have grown

A low growl rumbled through the crowd. One man, his face contorted with fear and anger,

“To approach honest, god-fearing humanity so casually, so boldly! But its disguise won’t fool

Another man beside him moved. Then three more. Then five. A small, angry vanguard
detaching itself from the crowd, advancing slowly, purposefully, towards the small, frozen

What…what did I do wrong…?

The cobblestones beneath here feet gave the faintest twitch.
Just once.
A little shiver. Almost nothing.
Monster
That cursed word. Again.

"Behold! The demon's mask slips!" The preacher's voice, triumphant even in chaos. "It bends

The sounds crescendoed. Screaming stone. Shrieking metal. Sobbing humans.
Finally, *** couldn't bear not knowing anymore.
Her eyes cracked open.
The world had gone wrong.
Where smooth cobblestones had been, the street now wept black ichor through widening
cracks. The viscous fluid moved wrong—upward, defying gravity, forming reaching tendrils
that grasped at nothing. The drain grate had twisted inward like a screaming mouth, and from
its depths came wet breathing sounds too large to be human.
The walls... oh, the walls. Masses of glistening ebony flesh pushed through brick and mortar
like diseased fruit. Not plant, not animal, but something between—surfaces that pulsed with
veins that shouldn't exist, sprouting thorns that wept red tears. They branched and twisted
skyward, reaching with too many joints, like fingers growing fingers growing fingers.
And the humans—
The dock worker who'd brandished his cargo hook now stared at his empty hand. The hook
lay on the ground, a puddle of molten metal still steaming. His face had gone the color of
spoiled milk, mouth working soundlessly as he backed away.
The factory worker's wrench had sprouted tiny legs and scuttled into the shadows like a metal
spider. He'd fallen to his knees, making the sign of the cross over and over, whispering
prayers that came out as whimpers.
The washerwoman's paddle had become driftwood, soft and rotten, crumbling in her grip.
She dropped it and ran, her scream high and thin.
Most were already fleeing. A young boy was dragging the older washerwoman away, both of
them stumbling over each other in their haste. Others pushed and shoved, climbing over
fallen companions, dignity forgotten in primal terror.
Only the preacher remained steady, standing on his crate like a captain on a sinking ship. His
eyes blazed with terrible joy.
"See how it reveals itself! See how the mask crumbles! This is what dwells among us,
wearing the faces of our children!"*** looked down at her small hands. Still human. Still pale and soft and five-fingered. The
white dress still white, the dark jacket still neat. She looked exactly as Yhounden had made
her—pretty, innocent, harmless.
But around her, reality screamed otherwise.
A cold truth clicked into place, sharp as winter glass.
They were afraid.
Because of her.
Because she was doing exactly what a monster does.
"…S-sorry…" The word tore itself from her lips in a broken whisper, barely audible above
the distant screams and the strange hissing, cracking sounds emanating from the street itself.
"I'm… s-sorry…!"
Then she turned. And ran. Ran blindly, desperately, away from the crowd, away from the
preacher, away from the monstrous things her own terror was apparently creating.
"Do not let it escape!"
The preacher bellowed after her retreating form even as, behind him, the glass of a nearby
shop window suddenly shattered inward, the shards twisting mid-air, curling unnaturally like
black petals closing protectively over a bleeding, unseen eye.
"That thing is nothing but a blight! An agent of the Bottomless Pit! If we let it flee now, it
will surely devour this city whole!"
But his remaining audience was no longer listening to him. They were fleeing too.
Her Mary Janes slapped against wet stone—splash-crack-splash-crack—each impact sending
shocks up her small legs. Human bodies weren't meant to run like this. Her chest burned,
lungs trying to suck in air that wouldn't come fast enough. Her side developed a stitch, sharp
as a knife between ribs she'd only recently learned she had.
Behind her, a lamp post twisted into a corkscrew. A postbox split open like a metal flower,
letters spilling out and transforming into paper moths that burst into black flame. A puddle
she splashed through began boiling, steam rising in shapes that might have been hands.
She didn't see any of it. Just ran.
Around a corner. Her shoulder clipped brick, spinning her sideways. She stumbled, caught
herself, kept going. Down another alley. Through a courtyard where laundry lines snapped
and writhed like serpents. Past a church where the iron gate groaned and bent outward,
reaching—
But *** didn't see.She only ran.
And ran.
And ran.
Snot mixed with tears, making breathing even harder. Her throat felt like she'd swallowed
glass. Each heartbeat hammered against her ribs like something trying to escape.
Streets blurred together. Left. Right. Straight. No pattern, no plan.
Just away.
*** ran until her knees wobbled like jelly. Until the world smeared into watercolors—grey
stone bleeding into yellow gaslight bleeding into black sky. Her human eyes couldn't focus
anymore. Everything doubled, tripled, melted.
Until her breath came in whistles and wheezes, each gasp feeling like drowning in air.
Until—
Silence.
No voices. No screams. No sounds of twisting metal or groaning stone.
Just her own ragged breathing, harsh as a saw through wood.
She slowed—one stumbling step—then another—then her legs simply quit.  Her back hit a
wall hard enough to knock out what little air remained. Rough brick caught her dress as she
slid down, down, down until cold stone met her bottom.
Another alley. Narrow. Dark. A dead end with walls so close she could touch both sides if she
stretched. Crates stacked high. The smell of rotting vegetables and something worse. No gas
lamp here. Just shadows and the faint, distant glow of the city beyond.
"I'm sorry…"
She brought her trembling legs up against her chest, wrapping her arms around them. Making
herself small. Smaller. If she could just shrink enough, maybe she'd disappear completely.
"… I didn't mean to… I was just…I wanted..."
Tears came properly now. Not the pretty tears from storybooks but ugly, body-shaking sobs
that hurt coming out. Her nose ran. Her eyes swelled. Each sob felt like it might crack her
borrowed ribs.
"My fault…it's all my fault…"
Mother and Father were right. They knew best all along. Her very existence was a mistake, a
cosmic error, a blight. That's why they cast her away. That's why everyone left. That's whyPa-lach would leave too, if she knew what *** truly was.
If she wasn't already gone. If the red flower hadn't already bloomed fully while *** wasted
time trying to pretend she could help.
Slowly, *** lifted her tear-streaked face and looked down at her hands lying limply in her
lap. These pale, small, pretty little human hands. Five fingers each. Soft palms. Tiny
fingernails. All fake. All lies. Her true limbs were ugly, writhing tentacles that broke
everything they touched.
She clenched them into fists, glaring at them through her tears.
Stupid! Stupid, ugly thing!
Thinking being like a princess, even just looking like one, was ever possible!
*** should've known. No matter how human it dressed.
No matter how much it tried.
A lie will remain a lie.
"I can only ruin things…"
A drop of something dark splashed onto her white dress. She looked up through blurred
vision. The brick wall behind her wept black tears from invisible wounds. Even here, even
trying to be still and small and nothing, she corrupted.
The sobs solidified, hardening into snarls, raw and wounded.
You're just a monster…!
And there is only one ending for monsters…!
*** began to rake her own fingernails, small and sharp, harshly against the pale surface of
her forearms. Again and again, dragging the points across her flesh.
One stroke.
Then another.
And another.
Skin split like paper. Red welled up in perfect lines, following the paths she'd drawn. The
blood looked so human. Moved like the illustrations in fairy tales when princes were
wounded. Dark red rivulets slid over the wrists, dripping onto the stone floor in fat, wet
splats.
Yet, the stinging pain, the blooming blood… In stories, the monster died when the sword
pierced its heart. One strike. Clean. Final.This is not enough…!
*** clawed harder. Raked furiously, back and forth, until raw welts blossomed down both
arms, until the skin bubbled up with shredded edges. The pain built in layers—first sharp,
then burning, then a deep throb that matched her heartbeat. But still her body persisted. Still
her lungs pulled air. Still her heart pumped blood.
Not enough….!
With a wild groan of utter desperation, *** abandoned the scratching and brought her
trembling palms up, pressing them hard against her own face, thumbs digging brutally
towards her eyes. Like plucking out the magic gems that gave golems life. Remove the eyes,
stop the monster.
But her hands were too small. Her thumbs too short. The angle all wrong. This child body
refused to break itself properly, protecting what she desperately wanted to destroy.
Defeated, sobbing renewed, she let her hands fall away, slumping back against the cold brick
wall. Blood from her arms smeared across her face, mixing with tears and snot. She could
taste copper on her lips.
It was then, as her blurry gaze drifted downward, that something glinted faintly in the gloom.
Metallic. Lying scattered just inches from where she sat—small, sharp shapes. Two of them.
Sleek, dark metal, wickedly pointed.
Like tiny swords. Like the answer to a prayer she hadn't known she was praying.
*** stared at them for a long moment, her sobbing slowly subsiding into shuddering breaths.
She wiped her nose with her sleeve—it came away red and wet. Then, pushing herself up
onto shaky legs that barely held, she staggered forward and knelt.
Her small fingers closed around the cool handle. Heavier than expected. The weight of real
metal, not story-picture swords. She stood slowly, holding the dagger up to catch what little
light filtered into the alley.
It reminded her of the beautiful swords Grimi carried in the illustrations. The ones that
always found the monster's heart. Just… smaller. Child-sized. Perfect.
She tested the edge against her fingertip. Barely a touch, but the blade bit immediately. A
bead of red welled up, joining the mess already coating her hands. Sharp. Properly sharp. Not
like her useless fingernails.
Yes. This will be enough. It must…!
Taking a deep, shuddering breath that caught on broken sobs, *** brought the point up to her
