arms. She tilted her head back, exposing the pale throat Yhounden had shaped so carefully.
So human. So fragile.Just like in the stories. The hero's blade finds the monster's weak spot. One thrust. The beast
falls. Everyone celebrates. The end.
She pressed slightly. The point dimpled her skin, then broke through. A single drop of
warmth trickled down her neck. It would be so easy. Push harder. Let the blade slide home.
Finally cleanse the horrible sin of her existence. Finally free everyone from the troublesome
little monster that brought only fear and ruin.
She closed her eyes. Gathered her will.
Just one push. Quick and clean. Like the stories.
The perfect ending for a monster.
But right in that final moment, before she could commit the act, she took one last,

Pa-lach.

If *** died here—no. Wait.
Can't die.

Which meant she'd been sitting here bleeding, crying, being dramatic while Pa-lach—
While Pa-lach was actually dying.

Stupid stupid stupid wasting time on impossible things while she—

Her cursed powers.

"…Dramatic little brat…!" The blonde girl rasped out after a moment. "Told you… I was
just….resting my eyes…"
Even as she spoke, her hand came up – slowly, uncertainly, like she wasn't quite sure what to
do with it. It hovered for a moment before settling awkwardly on ***'s trembling back.
I did it… I saved her…!
*** pressed her face harder into Pa-lach's shoulder, nuzzling her tear-streaked cheeks against
the rough wool. The blonde's warmth seeped into her, steady and real, chasing away the
hollow feeling that had settled in ***'s chest after using her power.
So maybe… maybe I'm not a monster after all…?
It was like a stone lifting from her soul, a weight she hadn't even known she carried—Maybe I can be good… maybe Dad and first Mother will come back for me… maybe—
A pair of hands pressed against her shoulders, gentle but insistent, creating distance.
"Alright… I think… that's enough, Malýshka…"
As *** reluctantly released her hold and stepped back, Pa-lach shifted against the wall. One
hand braced against grimy brick as she prepared for the deep ache that should have bloomed
from her side.
"And sorry for before… I shouldn't have lost my cool…"
She took a slow breath, waiting. But the expected pain was... absent.
"What the…?"
Her brow furrowed. Where there should have been fire spreading from the wound, there was
only the general soreness of a hard day.
She pressed her palm firmly against her side through the jacket. Nothing beyond tenderness,
like pressing on a week-old bruise. Her frown deepened as she shifted her weight, testing.
The weakness she'd felt creeping through her limbs, the cold spreading from blood loss – all
vanished.
"This… this can't be….I remember…"
It was then, as her gaze snapped to ***, that she saw them properly. Angry red lines
decorated the child's forearms.
Her expression shifted, concern cutting through confusion. She reached out, taking one of
***'s small arms with practiced gentleness, turning it to see the full damage. The scratches
ran in parallel lines, some deeper than others, all deliberate.
"Malýshka..." Her voice carried that particular sharpness of someone who'd seen too much.
"What is this? Who did this to you?"
Without waiting for an answer, she scanned the alley with hunter's eyes. Her gaze caught on
metal glinting in the dim light – her daggers, laying on the cobblestones where they'd fallen.
Moving carefully, still unconsciously protecting her side despite the absence of pain, she
retrieved them. The blade *** had held showed only a whisper of blood near the tip. Her eyes
tracked from the mostly-clean steel to the child's ravaged arms, to the small hands with their
ruined fingernails.
She secured them back inside the pocket with seasoned precision and looked back at ***,
something complicated lived in her grey eyes – gratitude tangled with worry.
"….What happened while I was out?"
*** looked down at her scratched arms, then up at Pa-lach's waiting face.Explain the truth?
Explain the horrible thoughts, the despair, the desperate need to destroy this human shell, the
failure even at that?
Explain that she'd clawed at herself like a trapped animal?
How could she possibly make this human understand? Pa-lach had saved her, helped her,
gotten hurt for her... the least *** could do was not burden her further, not reveal the
monstrous, self-destructive thing she truly was inside.
No. She had to lie.
Fumbling in her pocket, she retrieved the damp notebook and pen once more. The leather
cover felt clammy against her palm, pages slightly warped from earlier puddles. She quickly
scribbled, forcing her handwriting to remain steady despite the tremor in her hand, before
turning the notebook around.
'Shadow wolves with glass teeth came from the walls. I used the shiny sharp thing to chase
them back into the dark.'
Pa-lach read the note, her expression unreadable for a moment. Then, her brow narrowed
down just slightly and without taking her eyes off ***, she reached out again, firmly taking
hold of one of ***'s small wrists with her other hand.
"Shadow wolves, you say?" Pa-lach murmured, turning ***'s hand over, gently but
insistently exposing the small fingers, pointing pointedly at the damaged, blood-flecked
fingernails.
"Strange sort, then. Were these mystical beasts perhaps fonder of chewing on your fingernails
than biting your flesh?"
Caught. The lie unraveled with such simple logic. Heat flooded ***'s cheeks, spreading down
her neck like spilled paint. Her stomach twisted into knots – that peculiar human sensation of
shame making her insides feel too tight. She immediately turned her gaze downward, staring
fixedly at the grimy cobblestones, unable to meet Pa-lach's knowing eyes.
The blonde girl held her gaze on ***'s bowed head for another long moment, then let out a
slow, weary sigh.
"nu i nu…"
She released ***'s wrist and rubbed the bridge of her nose with two fingers before pressing
the notebook firmly back against ***'s chest. The force made *** stumble back a half-step as
Pa-lach straightened up, rising from her crouch with only the slightest favoring of her left
side.
"Whatever….The important thing is you're... mostly... safe and sound."*** caught the notebook against her chest with a soft "mfh…!" The worn leather felt warm
now from Pa-lach's hands, grounding her. She curled her small fingers around its familiar
edges, then quietly slid it back into her coat pocket as the blonde girl's gaze flicked down the
dark street they had been heading towards before everything fell apart.
"Right. Change of plan."
Her gaze swept the immediate vicinity, cataloging exits and dangers with practiced ease.
"Let's go. There should be a place nearby where we can get some bandages for those arms."
She glanced back at *** pointedly. "Wouldn't be good showing up to find your 'mom' looking
like you've been wrestling alley cats, now would it?"
She didn't wait for an answer but gave a small nod with her chin, indicating *** should
follow.
But *** didn't move immediately. Her feet felt rooted to the spot like a cursed tree.
She's mad….. Because I was stupid. Because I disobeyed….
The blonde girl took two steps, then stopped, sensing the lack of small footsteps beside her.
She turned back, one eyebrow raised slightly, finding *** still standing hesitantly near the
alley wall, head bowed like a wilted flower.
"Well?" Are you coming?"
*** flinched at the sharp tone.
"Or maybe," Pa-lach added, tilting her head slightly—shifting her voice to something almost
playful. "You're just planning how to latch onto my leg again the moment I turn my back,
hm?"
The unexpected tease cut through ***'s spiral like sunlight through storm clouds. She looked
up sharply, eyes wide. Pa-lach wasn't shouting. She wasn't wearing that terrifying cold mask
anymore. Her expression was still guarded, weary, but the sharp edges had softened into
something that might have been fond exasperation.
She didn't hate her. She wasn't going to leave her behind for being broken.
Relief crashed through *** like a wave, making her knees weak and her chest feel suddenly
too full. Her throat closed up with that strange human thing where happiness and leftover fear
got tangled together. A tiny, trembling smile found its way to her lips.
Taking the hint, she hurried forward, almost tripping over her own feet in her haste. Her small
hand reached out, finding the familiar rough fabric of Pa-lach's dark skirt, fingers curling into
the hem like it was the only solid thing in the world.
Only then, feeling that small, trusting tug, did the blonde girl turn and finally march forward
again into the London night.Chapter End Notes

"…clean bandages, tincture of arnica, and powdered alum…."
Pa-lach stated curtly, cutting through the relative quiet of the small chemist's shop she'd
steered *** into just off the main thoroughfare near the bridge. Her voice carried a slight rasp
—hours of walking from Piccadilly showing in each word.
"…Do you have them?"
A moment's pause, then a calm female voice replied from beyond a tall wooden counter
stacked with glass bottles.
"Aye. Got all three, fresh stock."
The shopkeeper emerged—a sturdy woman with short black hair touched by grey, wiping her
hands on a canvas apron stained dark green and yellow from years of mixing remedies.
"Alum and arnica are on the shelves here, bandages I keep wrapped fresh in the back. Follow
me, if you please."
She turned toward tall shelves half-hidden by a heavy curtain. Pa-lach followed, and ***
noticed how her hand drifted to her side where the wound had been, fingers pressing against
the fabric as if checking it was really gone.
*** lagged behind, staring at the crowded shelves. So many bottles—some cloudy with
powders like crushed fairy wings, others dark as the potions stepmothers used. Labels curled
at their edges: Tinct. Opii, Sal Volatile, Lint & Plaster. Bundles of lavender and chamomile
hung from the rafters, brushing Pa-lach's hair as she ducked through.
The air tasted of so many things all at once *** couldn't help but wrinkle her nose—bitter
herbs, sharp chemicals that might make princesses sleep forever or wake them with a kiss.
"Bandages, bandages... not these heavy ones... no, not that drawer either…"
One jar caught ***'s attention—small, dull green, round like the apple Snow White bit.
Something so pretty couldn't truly hurt, could it?
"…Oh! There it is!" The shopkeeper exclaimed, plucking a brown bottle from a shelf. "That's
one down!"
***'s fingers reached out. The cool glass felt nice—
"Ah-ah ah!"The shopkeeper's hand caught her wrist, quick but gentle. For just a second, the woman
blinked hard, as if the room had tilted.
"Better not touch that one, sweetie~" She shook her head slightly. "That's potassium
permanganate solution. Wonderful for infection, but it'll stain those pretty hands purple and
burn."
*** pulled back, confused. Why did humans fear their own potions? In stories, the dangerous
things were always the most interesting—spinning wheels, magic mirrors, poisoned combs.
"You really can't stop chasing danger, can you, Malýshka?" Pa-lach's voice was tired. She
gave ***'s shoulder a light pat, steering her away from the shelf.
"Honestly..." She muttered, that hand going to her ribs again. "You're worse than a moth
drawn to flame…"
*** tilted her head. Moths? What were moths? Like butterflies' ugly sisters?
She hurried after them as the shopkeeper gathered supplies: white bandages, soft lint, and a
dark bottle that might hold sleeping wine.
"Right then! Everything you need," the shopkeeper said. Her gaze fell on ***'s scratched
arms and her expression softened. "Poor little mite... Who would—"
She stopped herself. Church bells tolled seven o'clock somewhere outside.
"If you'll just come this way to settle up…"
As Pa-lach turned to follow, *** spotted another shelf—tall vials of pale liquid like bottled
moonlight. Her finger reached toward a honey-golden one—
"Ahem!"
*** jumped at Pa-lach's sharp sound, pulling her hand back. The vial swayed slightly on its
shelf.
"Stay. Still." Pa-lach said each word carefully. "Do not touch anything else. Ponimayesh?"
*** met her stern gaze, then nodded, looking down at the floorboards.
"Good."
After another moment—making sure the warning stuck—Pa-lach turned around. ***
watched her reach the counter, fixing her gaze dutifully on her companion. From the front
came low murmurs.
"...This much? For simple bandages and wash?"
"Good arnica don't come cheap, my dear... not like some watered-down rubbish...""...Trying to fleece me because I have a child with me...?"
Minutes stretched, marked by tense murmuring and the steady tick-tock of a large clock
somewhere deeper in the shop. Boredom crept in—swift and complete as only a child could
feel it.
*** shifted from foot to foot. Swung her arms. Puffed her cheeks. Scuffed her Mary Jane
against the floorboard.
Waiting was horrible. Worse than the void's silence, because here interesting things sat just
out of reach!
Her gaze drifted to the shelves.
Pa-lach had said 'don't touch anything.'
A sly grin tugged at ***'s lips.
She'd said nothing about 'looking.'
Hands clasped behind her back like the serious adults in storybooks, *** began wandering
the narrow paths between shelves. She drank in every sight.
Jars of dried herbs giving off dusty scents. Ceramic pots with thick salves. Metal instruments
she couldn't guess the purpose of. Rolled papers tied with string—recipes? Spells? She
paused at each interesting label:
"Extract of Willow Bark,"
"Oil of Cloves,"
"Powdered Sulphur."
In larger jars, pale things floated in clear liquid. Twisted roots that seemed to shift when she
wasn't looking directly. A coiled snake that might have twitched. Something that looked like
tiny pickled eyeballs—did they follow her as she passed?
The round-bellied bottles distorted her reflection in funny ways. One made her head
enormous; another stretched her face long. She stifled giggles at each silly image.
Then she found them—blue crystals in a jar, shimmering like captured starlight.
Are these souls? she wondered, leaning closer. Yhounden had told her souls are like tiny
flames, but maybe... maybe she forgot they can came in different shapes? Blue crystal souls
instead of white fire ones?
"Oi, look at 'er—proper pretty, ain't she?"
Voices drifted through the thin walls from outside. Men's voices, rough and pleased."Fancy a bit o' fun, love?"
"Fufu~ You honour me, dear gentlemen."
*** froze. Her heart slammed against her ribs so hard she thought it might break free.
That voice. That exact way of saying 'fufu~'
Mom?
"Let's head 'cross the way—know a spot with more privacy..."
The voices faded with boot steps and laughter, moving away from the shop.
*** found herself pressed against the wall without remembering moving, ear searching for
more of that familiar sound. Her hands trembled against the wood.
No. Mom's looking for me. She's worried. She wouldn't be... wouldn't be playing games with
strange men on the street.
Just some stupid female human who sounds like her. That's all.
The blue crystals in their jar had gone dark, as if a cloud passed over them.
"Malýshka....?"
Pa-lach's voice cut through the shop's quiet, calling from the front counter.
*** spun around, startled from her dark thoughts—too fast. Her elbow knocked hard against
the shelf. A tall glass jar filled with dried lavender wobbled, teetered, then crashed to the
floor with a sickening shatter.
Glass sprayed outward. Purple buds scattered like fallen soldiers across the floorboards.
*** froze, hands flying to her mouth, black eyes wide with horror.
"Malýshka..."
The blonde called again, weary now, that tone mothers used with troublesome children.
"…Stop playing and come out on your own now….or else…"
Panic flared hot in ***'s chest. Pa-lach was already annoyed.
If she saw this mess… she might get truly angry.
…Angry enough to leave?
*** dropped to her knees among the lavender and glittering shards. Petals stuck to her palms
as she scooped them desperately. She spotted a large curved piece—like a puzzle edge—and
reached for it.Pain bloomed. A red bead welled on her fingertip.
She bit back a whimper and kept working. Both hands gripped one shard, pressing it against
another, trying to match the breaks.
They fit perfectly.
Until—
clack!
They didn't.
The pieces clattered apart.
Why won't they stay?
She heard footsteps from the front—faint but approaching.
The shapes match! They should stick!
It doesn't make sense!
Her jaw clenched as she pressed the shards together again. And again.
They fell apart. Over and over.
Fine. If the world refused to cooperate…
I’ll make it…!
*** closed her eyes tight, picturing the jar whole, smooth, seamless. A faint hum rose as
glass scraped against wood, pieces shifting like beetles.
But what if it breaks again?
The sliding sounds intensified
—clink, clank—
—clank, clink—
And the flowers… how will anyone know what's inside?
—then stopped.
She opened her eyes. The jar stood whole, filled with lavender again. Near the base, a tiny
glass lavender sprig had been added—delicate as frost, catching the light.
Done!Pure triumph flooded through her tiny body. She bounced on her toes, fists clenched in silent
celebration, like a Grimi dancing over a dragon’s corpse.
Another victory! Proof I'm not a monster!
No monster could fix what they broke.
Beaming with pride, she lifted the jar reverently with both hands, as if presenting a holy grail
—
"Found you…"
The voice sliced through her joy. Low. Cold.
Terrifyingly close.
Right behind her.
***'s pupils shrunk to slits, body jolting so hard the jar nearly slipped from her hands.
How…?
I didn't even hear her steps…!
"What part of 'don't touch anything' didn't you get?"
Before *** could stammer, before she could even turn—
The hands came first.
Pain bloomed like fire on both sides of her head as Pa-lach's fingers closed around her
earlobes—the exact grip a mother would use for her rebellious brat, practiced and merciless.
A strangled squeak escaped *** as the jar slipped from her numb fingers.
But instead of shattering, it bounced with a soft thump. Twice. Thrice. Then rolled unharmed
against a shelf leg.
The blonde’s eyes tracked the jar's path, a slight furrow between her brows at its resilience.
But she said nothing, simply turned and began walking toward the front counter, ***'s
earlobe still firmly pinched.
*** yelped, tears pricking her eyes. She tried to make her ear stop hurting, tried to think it
better like she'd fixed the jar—but the pulsing pain shattered her focus before she could even
begin.
"Stop whining. If you didn't want your ear pulled…"
Another sharp tug.
"...You should have listened and behaved. Simple as that."The shelves passed in a blur of bottles and shadows. The lavender scent seemed to follow
them, clinging to ***'s dress where she'd knelt in the scattered buds.
"Now move," She continued, her grip never loosening, “unless you want me to pull harder
and really give you something to cry about."
*** quickened her stumbling pace, one hand reaching up instinctively toward the captured
ear before dropping—she knew better than to try prying away those iron fingers. This was
her punishment for touching after being told not to.
Just like Goldilocks got chased by bears for touching their things.
Except Pa-lach's fingers were much worse than any bear.
 
 
"Sorry for the interruption," the blonde girl said curtly as she reached the counter, ***
stumbling beside her with head bowed, rubbing her throbbing ear.
The shopkeeper cleared her throat, color rising in her cheeks—that particular embarrassment
of witnessing familiar domestic scenes. She'd heard the ear-pulling, the scolding. Just another
wayward child and strict guardian.
"No harm done," the shopkeeper managed, busying herself with wrapping the supplies. “As I
was saying, That'll be….let me see….two shillings sixpence for the lot."
"One shilling eight. The arnica's half-empty and the carbolic's been watered."
The shopkeeper's mouth opened, then closed. After a moment's consideration: "Two shillings
even, and that's being generous."
"Done." The blonde placed the coins on the counter with a decisive click.
As the shopkeeper gathered the purchases, she glanced at *** slumped in defeat, then back to
the blonde girl. "Perhaps... go a bit softer on your little sister, shall you? She seems—"
"Suffering builds character." The response came sharp as winter wind. "Better she learns
discipline now than grows soft and useless later."
The shopkeeper's lips thinned but she said nothing more, simply pushing the wrapped bundle
across the counter.
Pa-lach steered *** toward a quieter corner near the back, where a sturdy wooden chair sat
beside a small table holding earthenware jars. The gaslight barely reached here, casting long
shadows that made the bottles on nearby shelves gleam like watching eyes.
"Hold still."The blonde pushed *** down onto the hard chair before turning to unwrap the bundle. Clean
linen bandages, lint, dark carbolic wash, powdered alum, and arnica salve spread across the
table.
"The shopkeeper won't let us overstay our welcome..." The blonde girl unscrewed the
carbolic vial, the sharp chemical scent cutting through the herb-laden air. "So please behave...
for once, and let's get this over quickly."
She pulled up a stool, sitting heavily. Taking ***'s arm firmly, she extended it despite the
child's instinctive tensing. The angry red scratches crisscrossed pale skin like the witch's
curse-marks from ***'s stories.
First, cleaning.
The blonde dipped cloth into water from an earthenware jar. The cool water initially soothed,
but friction against raw scratches made *** wince. She held still though, watching Pa-lach
work with steady efficiency—hands that had probably done this countless times before.
The clock ticked loudly in the quiet corner. Nine twenty-three. Late for a child to be out. Late
for shopping. Late for everything except trouble.
"This'll sting... get ready."
The blonde girl moistened a pad and sprinkled white alum powder on it. *** watched the
powder fall like poisoned snow from her fairy tales, not knowing what to expect as it moved
toward her wrist.
Pain bloomed—sudden, sharp, like dragon's breath licking at raw flesh.
*** yanked her arm back, cradling it protectively against her chest.
Pa-lach's eyes closed briefly, then rolled toward the ceiling—patience worn thin as old cloth.
"I know, I know... it's painful, Malýshka..." Her voice carried that flat lecturing tone. "...But
sometimes... no, most of the time, a little pain now prevents much worse later."
The blonde tilted her head, coolly gauging ***'s tearful expression.
"...You do still want to get back to your mom, right?"
*** sniffled, rubbing her eyes with her uninjured hand. Her lips wobbled before she nodded,
small, obedient motions that said everything.
"Then give me back your arm."
The blonde girl extended her palm, waiting. "The sooner we finish, the sooner we can get
back searching for her."
A heartbeat's hesitation.Then—slowly—*** placed her small wrist in Pa-lach's larger palm.
"Thank you."
The blonde's fingers closed secure around the wrist. She pressed the stinging powder to each
scratch, ignoring ***'s soft hisses as the antiseptic burned like cursed spindle-pricks, clotting
the last blood-trickles.
Silence descended again, broken only by ***'s sharp little intakes of breath and the soft rustle
of Pa-lach's movements. *** couldn't help but ponder this slow, slightly torturous human way
of fixing things.
All this careful cleaning, painful stinging powders, soothing salves applied around the hurt...
it seemed so inefficient, so... primitive compared to the swift, seamless mending she had
performed on the blonde girl's side.
Yet another revelation of my virtues!
A flicker of pride warmed her despite the discomfort. For a fleeting second, she considered
simply... fixing her own arms right now. Willing the scratches closed, the skin whole again,
just like she'd done for Pa-lach.
It would be so easy.
But the thought died as quickly as it arose, extinguished by a cold wave of fear. The wide
eyes, the open mouths, the raw terror twisting the orphans' faces when the cake appeared
from nowhere. The way they had scrambled away, shrieking "Monster!"
What if Pa-lach reacted like that?
What if seeing ***'s power used so blatantly, even just on herself, flipped some switch?
*** couldn't risk it.
Not now.
Not ever.
I'll tell her... later. At the—
"You know..." The blonde girl suddenly murmured, not looking up from her task. "If this is
truly bothering you so much... you could have just fixed these yourself. Like you fixed my
side."
—right time....
*** froze completely, every muscle locking rigid. Her breath caught.
She knew?! All along?!But how?! When?!
Pa-lach, sensing the sudden stillness, paused and glanced up.
"What's that face for, Malýshka?" She inquired drily, though a tiny smirk touched the corner
of her lips as she picked up fresh cloth for the arnica. "Wondering how I figured out your
secret?”
Without waiting for an answer—not that *** could have given one with her throat closed
tight as a fist—the blonde dipped the cloth and dabbed gently around the next gash.
"I just pondered things....Connected the dots... Added up the few scarce hints you've
dropped..."
She moved to another scratch near ***'s wrist.
"First... those drawings. You tried to draw yourself, barely managed a shaky stick figure with
a triangle skirt." A pause as she applied more arnica. "Then later, you sketched your 'mom'
with the skill of a master artist. That leap... it's not natural.”
She finished with that arm, gently setting it down before picking up the left.
"Then there's how you behave... Like everything's new. Childish curiosity I thought at first,
but yours is too extreme." Her gaze flickered briefly upward. "Oh, and the milk. I've never
seen before a child look THAT happy over a simple cup."
The blonde reached for the linen bandage, beginning to unwind a strip.
"And then..." Her voice dropped slightly, focusing on wrapping the bandage snugly around
***'s forearm. "That glass jar before."
She nodded toward the shelf where the jar rested intact, the tiny glass lavender sprig catching
the light.
"Should have shattered. And that delicate little addition?" She secured the first wrap. "No one
adds artistic flourishes to storage jars in back-alley shops."
"All of that..." She smoothed the bandage flat. "And knowing the Lord above never bothers
with such direct interventions... well..."
A sideways glance at ***.
"That only leaves you."
She finished the knot, reaching for more bandage.
*** lowered her gaze, watching numbly as white linen covered the angry marks on her right
arm. She flexed her bandaged fingers. The blonde girl's hands had been surprisingly gentle.
So she knew.Knew *** wasn't normal, knew about the strange powers, knew about the healing.
Yet... she was still here. Hadn't screamed or run.
Still...
What if something had changed inside? What if she was thinking the same as the others did?
That *** didn't belong?
What if she was just better at hiding it?
*** couldn't stand not knowing.
With her bandaged right hand she fumbled for the damp notebook in her pocket. The pages
stuck together as she pulled it out, water damage making them crinkle. She dropped it on her
lap, then struggled with the pen, the bandages making it hard to grip the cap.
Finally managing to uncap it, she flipped through sticky pages until she found an empty
corner on the first available page. The pen scratched urgently across the warped paper, ink
bleeding slightly into the damp fibers.
Done. Using the same hand—her left still held by Pa-lach—she lifted the notebook and
gently bopped it against the blonde's nose.
She flinched at the unexpected poke, turning with a flicker of impatience.
"What now, Mal—" She paused, her expression softened with realization. "Oh, right, forgot.
Thanks for saving my life... anything else? Got your other arm to take care of..."
*** shook her head quickly, pointing down at the page with her index finger, tapping
insistently at the freshly written words:
'Do you think I'm a monster?'
The letters were shaky, pressed too hard in places where urgency overtook control.
Pa-lach's hands stilled.
Her eyes didn't move right away—shadows flickered in their depths. Then slowly she lifted
her gaze, holding *** with unwavering focus.
*** clenched her hands tightly around the edges of the notebook.
Resisting the urge to look away.
Curl to herself.
Disappear—
A breathy snort broke the tension.It escaped the blonde girl’s throat like something caught between disbelief and amusement.
Her lips twitched. Once. Twice. Then the rigid line broke, curving upward to show white
teeth.
*** blinked in bewilderment. She was ready, expecting the worst…not this?
"You? A monster?"
She shook her head, reaching for the bandage roll to resume work on ***'s left arm.
"You're no angel, that's for sure..." The bandage wound steadily. "You run off into danger
without thinking... touch things you're explicitly told not to..."
Her gaze flickered up pointedly.
"...definitely don't listen..."
***'s cheeks burned. She knew exactly what Pa-lach meant.
The blonde finished the left arm with a neat tug, immediately taking ***'s right wrist.
"But..."
Her voice lost its amusement, turning firm as she focused on the bandaging.
"...You're the last thing I'd call a monster."
She paused, securing the wrap, then added quietly, almost to herself:
"...Hell, between the two of us? I'm the one closer to being one."
*** raised a brow. Her? A monster? Surely a strange human jest.
But if I'm not a monster... then what am I?
Her black eyes widened, staring unseeing at the bandage now winding around her upper arm.
Pa-lach glanced up, reading the confusion on ***'s face.
"Yes, you're naive... curious to the point of being a hazard. Need to learn to listen..."
She met ***'s gaze directly.
"But apart from that? You're the most innocent, good-hearted little thing I've had the
misfortune of meeting in this cesspit of a city.”
Good of heart.
The words landed like a blessing. *** didn't grasp all the human terms—cute? adorable?—
but good of heart she understood. Like the heroes in her stories."So..." Pa-lach finished the last bandage, leaning back slightly. "If someone's been calling
you names, telling you you're worthless..."
She nodded at ***'s arms.
"...Telling you to hurt yourself, or worse..."
She leaned forward, pressing her gloved finger firmly against ***'s chest, right over the
beating heart.
"…Don't let anyone decide your path. Not now, not ever." Her voice hardened with
conviction. "The only one who gets to choose what you are…what you want to become…is
you."

She reached for the next strip of cloth—
—but paused.
Soft sniffles reached her ears.

The door shut behind them with a hollow clack. A breeze from the river drifted through the
narrow streets of North Southwark—crisp, cleaner than the rest of the city, carrying the scent
of water and coal smoke.
This stretch near the river crossing felt different from the deeper alleys they'd navigated
earlier. Buildings stood neater here, trimmed in brick and cast iron, windows scrubbed
enough to see through. Even the cobbles beneath their feet were less caked in filth.
Few souls wandered the streets; the working day long over, respectable folks now indoors.
A handful of late-night carriages moved lazily across the streets, iron-shod wheels rumbling
softly, guided by weary coachmen with dulled reins and half-shut eyes.
One driver, slumped forward on his seat, muttered to his equally tired horse:
"Just hold on, Bess… stay strong now… one last ride and we get back to the stable…"The blonde girl squinted ahead through the fog. Shapes crowded near Waterloo Bridge's
entrance—men, mostly. Bulky, broad-shouldered, the kind shaped by workhouses and
shipping yards.
But something was wrong with how they stood.
They weren't moving. Just... there. Some leaning against the stone, others standing in the
middle of the path, all perfectly still in the gaslight like they'd forgotten what they were
doing. No conversation, no shuffling—just figures arranged like someone had left them there.
"Yobanyye alkashi..."
Beside her, *** peered toward the bridge, frowning at one man who leaned far over the stone
balustrade—just like she had done on Westminster Bridge before Pa-lach yanked her back.
Why is he doing that?
Does he want to fall too?
But falling doesn't feel good…
They continued forward, shoes clicking against the broad road. The street widened here, large
enough for carriages to pass two abreast. The fog made everything past twenty feet into
suggestions of shapes.
Then *** saw her.
Far down the bridge, barely visible through the mist—a slender silhouette perched on the
balustrade. Dark hair falling long, too perfect even at this distance.
*** froze. Her lungs seized.
Yhounden.
Mom.
She's here! Waiting for me!
A squeak escaped her throat. She grabbed Pa-lach's wrist with both bandaged hands, tugging

trying to run forward, only Pa-lach's grip keeping her from bolting.

despite the child's attempts to rush ahead.

likely heading home after his last fare.

Just as the chaotic noise reached an unbearable crescendo, as the monstrous crowd closed in
to tear both *** and Agalika's corpse apart, as reality threatened to shatter completely—
Shhhrkkk—
Thin. Cold. Metallic.
Like a razor slicing silk.Instantly, the swirling, flaming shadows recoiled as if struck. Invisible lines, sharp as the
sound itself, seemed to bisect them cleanly. The writhing forms stuttered, fractured, then
dissolved rapidly into smoke and ash that scattered on the night wind.
Within seconds, they were gone. Only faint grey dust settled on the cobblestones where a
crowd had stood moments before.
Silence.
Only crying remained. Desperate. Piercing.
The unmistakable, heart-wrenching grievance of a little girl.
Slowly, hesitantly, like a creature emerging from a dark burrow after a storm, *** lifted her
tear-streaked face from Agalika's cold chest. She blinked, black eyes wide and disoriented,
trying to adjust to the sudden quiet. The street looked... normal again. Just carnage and ash.
Except for one thing—a faint glimmer.
A warmth, not from the ruined world around her, but from above.
*** lifted her face from Agalika's frost-touched skin, blood from her own scratches smearing
across her cheeks.
Her tear-blurred vision caught it—
A delicate, trembling sphere—no larger than her small hands could cup—drifting just feet
above Agalika's still form.
It wasn't pure white. Along its edges, darkness clung like smoke, writhing faintly against the
soft, flickering glow that danced like a tiny flame.
*** stared, mesmerized. The way it flickered, the way it burned without heat...
'Tiny pretty flames that burn inside every living thing,' Yhounden's voice echoed in her
memory. 'The only difference between is their flames are white, and they don't burn when you
touch them. They're what make the difference between a doll and a child, between wood and
life.'
"That's... that's her soul…?"
The sphere trembled, its tainted edges spreading slightly, like ink bleeding through wet paper.
Hope stirred in her battered heart.
If so….
Maybe…Trembling, *** lifted her palms toward it—slow, hesitant, terrified that if she moved too fast,
it would shatter like everything else she touched. Her bandages hung loose, unraveled and
stained with her own blood.
Her fingers inched closer.
The flame wavered gently, almost reaching back, then pulling away, as if uncertain.
So close.
Is mine like that too?
….Do monsters like me deserve one...?
Would mine look like this...?
Her fingertips were inches away now, trembling with effort and desperate hope—
Something tugged sharply at the back of her dress.
"That's enough, my child."
Before she could turn, she found herself being dragged backward, her small body scraping
painfully against rough cobblestones. The distance between her and Agalika grew with each
pull.
"NO!!"
*** shrieked, raw with anguish.
"NO, PLEASE!!"
"LET ME GO!!"
She left bloody handprints on the stones as she clawed for purchase. The soul began to rise,
slow and steady, the darkness at its edges pulsing.
"—DON'T TAKE HER FROM ME!!"
"I HAVE TO—I SWEAR—I CAN—I CAN—!"
But the soul kept ascending. Higher. Smaller. The tainted edges seeming to eat more of the
light.
"PLEASE...PLEASE…!"
Her dress tore against the cobblestones as she twisted, fighting the inexorable pull.
"I'LL BE GOOD!!"
"I'LL BE GOOD, I PROMISE, PLEASE LET ME TRY!""LET ME GO! LET ME DO THE RIGHT THING!!!"
She began to claw desperately at the hand gripping her collar, her own shadow writhing and
reaching toward the ascending soul with too many grasping tendrils.
"I CAN—"
"Sigh...You leave me no choice...."
A dull, heavy crack against the back of her skull.
White-hot pain exploded behind her eyes—
Her last sight was the glowing soul—now just a faint white dot being swallowed by endless
blackness—the corruption at its edges making it look like a dying star.
And then, nothing.
Her world turned instantly, mercifully, dark.
 
 
"As for you..."
Cold crystalline eyes drifted from ***'s limp, unconscious body—dangling weightless from
his grip by the scruff of her tattered dress—and slowly, inexorably, fixed onto the black-
haired woman standing beside him.
The man holding *** was tall, impeccably dressed despite the surrounding carnage. His pale
features remained perfectly composed, as if standing amid blood and destruction was merely
another Thursday evening. Only his eyes betrayed something beyond human—too sharp, too
knowing, seeing through dimensions rather than merely across them.
"'It will be all fine' uh...?"
"H-Hey now—"
Before she could continue, his free hand shot out with impossible speed, closing unerringly
around her earlobe, pinching hard between index finger and thumb.
"Eep—!!"
Yhounden squeaked, stumbling forward involuntarily. An awkward, strained expression
unbeknownst to her usual noble bearing plastered itself onto her pale face. Her grey eyes
darted sideways, seeking an escape route that didn't exist. The corners of her lips wavered,
curling into a weak grimace that pretended nonchalance but failed miserably."U-uh….Yoggy dearest? Could you please stop doing that scary face? And maybe let go too,
pretty please?" An uneasy chuckle escaped her. "I swear, I didn't plan for things to go THIS
bad!"
She gave a small, fluttering wave toward the devastation—the building with its carriage-sized
hole, the blood-stained cobblestones, the ash settling where a crowd had been, and there, in
the distance, a small still form with golden hair.
"Just... just wanted to give the child a taste of the ugly side of humanity, for character
development's sake!…Or whatever humans call that kind of thing."
Yog remained silent, simply holding her there, still piercing her with those crystalline eyes.
The air around him seemed colder, reality holding its breath.
Until finally he closed his eyes, exhaling a slow, bone-deep sigh.
"….Why should I even bother?" he muttered at last, almost to himself. "Scolding you is
pointless. Like always. My words'll simply go in one ear..."
He opened his eyes again, fixing her with a look of utter weariness.
"...and fly straight out the other."
Abruptly, he started moving forward, dragging the still-protesting, squeaking Yhounden
along mercilessly by her earlobe.
"Consider yourself 'grounded', Elk Goddess."
His other hand adjusted its grip on ***'s collar, and her small body—dress torn, bandages
unraveled and bloodied, scratches reopened—began bumping and scraping unceremoniously
over the dirty cobblestones behind him. Her head lolled with each pull, dark hair matted with
blood and ash.
"Perhaps a decade inside Shtranjii will finally teach you something resembling good
behavior. If nothing else, it will give me fewer headaches to deal with for the immediate
future."
Yhounden opened her mouth, no doubt ready to plead or bargain—
But at that moment, the air before them began to shimmer, then tear open silently. A swirling
vortex of pure blackness appeared in the middle of the road, pulsing faintly with internal,
non-Euclidean geometries. The portal's edges whispered with sounds that predated language,
and the smell of space between stars leaked out.
"W-Wait! What about the child?"
"Stop pretending." Yog replied coolly, never sparing her a glance. "It's clear from a light-year
away you don't care a shred about the child itself.""…Fair enough." Yhounden conceded instantly, smoothly shifting track. "But I mean,
seriously, if not me, who's going to handle it? Ensure it doesn't..."
She waved at the chaos around them—broken glass still falling, gas pipes hissing, a city
block traumatized.
"...become a walking catastrophe that will end up waking the 'idiot'? The whole reason you
gave us back then on why you chose to 'quarantine' the little one and its sibling, remember?"
Yog paused—just a hair's breadth from the portal's edge. The vortex pulled at debris near
their feet, hungry.
He looked down briefly at ***'s inert form. Blood dripped steadily from her scratches onto
the cobblestones, leaving a trail. Her small chest rose and fell, but otherwise she was still as
death—a broken doll being dragged to storage.
"….I know someone."
Without further explanation, he stepped forward into the black whirlwind. Yhounden, still
held fast by the ear, was pulled in immediately after with another undignified yelp. ***'s
small, limp body vanished silently into the vortex behind them, the last drops of her blood
marking where she'd been.
The portal shuddered violently once—like a living thing spitting venom at the world—before
collapsing inward with a sharp snap.
Silence returned to the blood-stained street. Flickering gaslights cast dancing shadows over
the devastation. Somewhere in the distance, a church bell tolled midnight.
And there, forgotten in the darkness, lay all that remained—
The remnants of a future that wasn't meant to be.

Sorry, Truly. Don't know what possessed me just now. Must be the spirit of Mary Sue
guiding me and my digital pen.
Anyway, with this chapter we have finally ended the victorian arc (You could argue
***'s innocence as well), hope you enjoyed the story and our protagonist's relationship
till it lasted, i tried to delay the inevitable. No I'm serious, this part of the story was the
first one i came up.
(Hell originally the first idea my two fantastic and very smart neurons managed to come
up was *** and Yhounden entering a bookshop to buy a new fairytale, *** seeing the
blonde girl coming out just besides them, realizing she looked so much like Red Riding
Rood, calling her out and then Bam! Whole incident scene)
The problem was tying a coherent progression leading to the aforementioned moment.
...Yah i know it's weird being happy after writing what is supposed to be a tragic
cliffhanger, feel free to call me a weirdo for that lol.
but i mean, for me it's like finally managing to take away that pesky little stone on my
shoes that refused to get out no matter how much i shake it, you know what i mean
right? RIGHT??
...Oh! Just one thing before leaving. So far i used 'alicespeak' font for ***'s and the
others's dialogues in order to convey their otherwordly nature and to make it lore
accurate. Tough i began to think that maybe it's not the best thing to make you pause the
reading and use the translator (Which btw is this one:https://zasz.su/bs/translator.html)
to even understand what is going.
So my question is: Do i keep using Alicespeak font? Or do i switch to normal english
like i did for Yhounden and Yog?
...In any case, like always, i hope you enjoyed the chapter, and if you have something to
say please feel free to comment, they do what catnip does to a cat.. And see ya next time
i come out from the basement to deliver a new chapters!Chapter 9: Empty Chrysalis

*** woke to a world still heavy with cloudlight.
Grey skies hung like damp linen, pressed flat against rooftops that leaned sleepily into each
other. The stones beneath her were cool and slick with morning dew, wrong against her cheek
—too soft, like skin pretending to be rock. Her face tingled where it had kissed the ground, a
thousand tiny needles pricking back to life.
She was lying on something hard—cobblestones, she thought—and a faint, persistent ringing
echoed in her ears.
Where am I?
With a groan, *** pushed herself up. The movement sent the world tilting sideways, and her
fingers found a tender, aching spot on the back of her skull. It pulsed with her heartbeat, each
throb a little reminder: you fell, you fell, you fell.
But… when?
Everything before was smudged, like illustrations left in the rain. There had been a street… a
bridge… Agalika.
A flash of memory, sharp and clear: the blonde girl in the middle of the road glancing back,
small smile on her lips as she, smelling of leather and that bitter plant—arnica—from the
chemist's shop.
"…And yours, Malýshka? Fair's fair. Spill your name too."
*** had been about to answer. For the very first time, someone wanted to know her name,
and she could finally tell them because Agalika had shared hers first.
The joy of it had bubbled up like—
Like what?
Why can't I remember?
A strange smell hung in the air, thick and cloying—iron mixed with something sweet, like the
roses in picture books but wrong, wilted, rotting from the inside. It coated her tongue, made
her want to spit.
Indistinct voices drifted from an alley mouth to her right. Low murmurs that rose and fell like
a prayer, or maybe a curse.She must be there.
As she pulled herself up and made her way there, something was wrong with the buildings.
They stood too tall, leaning over the road like giants peering down at an ant. Windows
watched her with black, empty eyes. The cobblestones beneath her feet felt squishy, giving
slightly with each step, as if the city itself was made of flesh pretending to be stone.
A thick crowd of humans stood clustered in a circle, breathing in perfect unison—in through
the nose, out through the mouth, like a single massive creature with many heads. Their voices
overlapped, creating a sound like wasps drowning in honey:
"…looks like something crawled up from below…"
"…Lord have mercy on this poor soul…"
"…children shouldn't see such things…"
Are they… talking about Agalika?
Her heart began to pound, each beat too loud, too fast, like something trying to escape her
chest. 
She ducked and pushed through the forest of human legs that went on forever. Being small
had its uses—she could slip between spaces that shouldn't exist, through gaps that closed
behind her like mouths.
"…a day of profound sadness, my brothers and sisters…"
A powerful voice rose above the murmur, deep and resonant. It tickled something in her
memory—but the voice kept sliding between tones.
Now it rumbled like Gothel as she sternly warned Rapunzel never to dare think about leaving
the tower, now it sang like The Kind Elk when she explained the moral of the fairy tales she
just read, now it rasped like dead leaves.
"We gather to bear witness… to a life halted by forces beyond comprehension…"
The crowd murmured agreement, but their words came out backwards, or maybe upside-
down.
"Sometimes...the greatest dangers walk among us in the most innocent of guises…"
*** finally pushed past the last pair of legs—
why were there so many, more than bodies, more than possible?
—and stumbled into the clear space at the center.
A carriage lay on its side like a dead beetle, wheels spinning slowly despite no wind. The
wood was splintered in patterns that reminded her of the frost on windows, spreadingoutward from a central point of impact.
Two horses lay still, but their positions kept shifting when she wasn't looking directly at them
—first sprawled naturally, then arranged like dolls, then back again.
And there, beside the wreckage, was Agalika.
She lay on her back, golden hair fanned out in a perfect circle like the halos in Mom's books
about angels. Her eyes were closed, her face peaceful. She looked like Sleeping Beauty
waiting for her prince, or Snow White in her glass coffin.
A wave of relief, warm and golden as honey, washed over ***.
She's just sleeping.
Just like Snow White.
She started forward, but then—
Pale daylight, then deep night with stars that screamed. The blood on the stones was there,
then gone, then there but dried brown, then fresh and spreading like spilled wine.
The carriage was upright—
—no, flipped—
—no, its wheel rested on something that looked like—
—like golden hair tangled in spokes—
It snapped back. Daylight. Stillness. The crowd breathing in unison, a single massive lung
expanding and contracting.
What… was that?
Something was wrong, wrong, wrong.
WRONG.
She swallowed—when had her throat gotten so dry, like she'd been screaming?—and
stumbled the last few steps.
"Agalika…?"
The blonde girl didn't stir.
She reached out, brushing her fingertips against the wool of Agalika's coat. For a heartbeat, it
felt warm, familiar. The next, cold as winter stones, cold as the void between stars.
"Wake up...You said you wanted to know my name. You can't sleep through that part..."She gave the shoulder a gentle shake, but a hand clamped down on her wrist. The grip was
firm but wrong—too many fingers, or maybe not enough. The knuckles bent in places they
shouldn't.
"That's enough, child."
She turned. The human with that ever changing voice loomed over her, but his face kept
sliding between expressions like he had too many of them fighting for space. His eyes burned
with the kind of light that shouldn't exist in daylight—the light of things that lived in the
spaces between spaces.
"Do you find this amusing? This mockery of the dead?"
“…Wh-what?  *** shook her head hard enough to make her vision blur.  “Sh-she’s not
dead!”
To prove it, to show these stupid humans who didn't understand how stories worked, she
gripped Agalika's shoulder harder, pulling to turn her over.
“She's just sleeping! I just need to—"
The body rolled with a wet, heavy sound that didn't belong to anything that had ever been
alive.
Where Agalika's face should have been was—
No.
Meat and bone and things that should stay inside. One eye stared at nothing, the other was
gone, leaving a dark hole that seemed to go down forever. The jaw hung loose, showing teeth
like scattered pearls in red silk.
Half her skull was caved in, brain matter glistening like—
Like the cake I made for those little hungry humans.
Memories flooded through.
Her own voice, trying to speak her name.
The horses screaming.
Windows shattering.
Agalika turning back with fear, never fear—just before the panicked horses—
Crack.
Crush.*** fell backwards. Agalika's body flopped back with the particular looseness of dolls with
cut strings. Her remaining eye rolled up, showing only white, and something dark and thick
leaked from her ear.
As she scrambled back, *** caught a flash of red on her arms. She looked down—the
bandages were gone.
When had they—?
The scratches she'd carved into herself glowed angry red, blood welling up fresh and hot,
dripping onto the not-quite-stones. Each drop sizzled slightly, as if the ground was hungry for
it.
"No—no no no—"
She crawled backward until her back hit something solid.
She looked up. Josiah stood there, but his kind face from the tavern was gone. This face was
cold as winter mornings, hard as the stones that shouldn't feel like flesh.
"You forgot already? What you did? The horror you brought upon her?"
She whimpered—a sound that wanted to be words but didn't dare—and scrambled away,
bumping into another chest.
"Ah, there's the little dove~"
Arms wrapped around her, hands aiming for her skirt, and she knew without looking it was
the longshoreman from the bridge, but his hands had too many knuckles and his breath
smelled like the bottom of the Thames where dead things dreamed.
"Look at those pretty cuts," someone cooed. "She's already started the good work!"
"Keep going, monster!"
"That's all you're good for—hurting things!"
"Hurt yourself like you hurt her!"
She tore away, her foot catching on something heavy.
The world tilted—
No, not again, not—
CRACK.
Her face met stone with the enthusiasm of old friends reuniting. Stars burst behind her
eyelids. Pain bloomed hot and wet from her nose. Through tears that tasted of copper and
salt, she saw what she'd tripped over.Agalika. Still there. Still wrong. Still wearing the ending *** had written for her without
meaning to. But now she looked different—first like Sleeping Beauty with her face caved in,
then Snow White with glass shards for a coffin, then just a girl who'd been kind to a monster
and paid the price.
The crowd closed in, their circle tightening like a noose made of wool and judgment. Aggie
with her pies that writhed like worms, the orphans with their clever fingers that had too many
joints, the men from the tavern who made those weird groans behind doors that now sounded
like dying.
But their faces kept slipping, showing things underneath. Not monsters—that would have
been better.
Monsters she understood. These were mirrors, each one reflecting back what she was.
Thing that breaks beautiful things.
Story-ender.
The wolf wearing girl-skin.
She crawled to Agalika's body, pressing her forehead against the cold shoulder, pulling it
close like a shield, like a treasure, like the last page of the last book in the world.
"Behold!" The Preacher's voice boomed from everywhere at once, from the stones, from the
sky, from inside her own skull. "It clings to its work! See how it embraces what it has
wrought!"
"Killer!"
"Demon!"
"Cut deeper!"
"Death to the monster!"
"The only good monster is a dead monster!"
The shadows were back. They always came back when the world got too sharp, too real, too
much. They leaked from the spaces between her thoughts, spreading across the cobblestones
like spilled ink mixed with blood—her blood, calling to them.
As they touched the crowd, they swelled and twisted. Encouragement grew crueler, more
specific:
"Drag those nails down to the bone!"
"You know you want to—you've tasted it before!"
"Join her! Join the pretty corpse!"Dozens became one, a deep guttural growl that came from everywhere and nowhere:
"This is what you are. This is all you'll ever be. Finish what you started."
The shadows reached the Preacher, who raised his arms to the grey sky that was too close,
too solid, like a ceiling made of old bones about to fall.
"Lord… You have shown us… the truth of abominations… things that wear the shape of
children… but inside—"
The darkness swirled, hungry, turning, reaching—
 
 
***’s eyes flew open.
The only sound was her own heart, rabbit-fast and painful against ribs that felt too small for
its frantic beating. Her chest rose and fell in quick, shallow pants—in-out-in-out—too fast,
like her body was trying to swallow all the air that existed in the universe.
Something warm and wet covered her skin, making her dress cling to her like it was trying to
become part of her. The fabric stuck unpleasantly to her arms, her chest, her legs—a second
skin she couldn't shed. It pooled in the hollow of her throat, trickled slowly down her spine.
Her trembling hands found smooth, infinite coldness beneath her. The surface stretched
endlessly in all directions, black and slightly transparent, like frozen smoke. Through it, far
below, she caught a glimpse of her own reflection staring back with wide, frightened grey
eyes.
Home.
Back in the void, the nowhere-place where she'd spent a thousand years alone before the kind
elk had found her.
Her panicked breathing began to slow almost against her will as the familiar, soul-deep
nothingness pressed in.
Just a dream. It was just a bad dream….
She pushed herself into a sitting position, looking down at her arms. The bandages were
there, the white cloth wrapped neat and careful from forearm to wrist, exactly as Palach—
Agalika—had done.
Some spots showed pink seeping through the white, like pale roses blooming under fresh
snow, but there were no fresh, gaping wounds from her frantic scratching. No crowd cheering
for her to hurt herself more.A dream shouldn’t hurt tough….
She pushed herself up fully, looking around again  for the one who always made the bad
things better.The Kind Elk's silhouette—either the towering antlered shape she'd first known
or the pretty human form she now wore—would be somewhere in the dark, wouldn't it? She
never left *** alone for long.
She'd be there with soft words and gentle touches and maybe with a new, lovely fairytale,
ready to explain what the terrible dream meant, why it hurt so much to remember—
To remember what?
Agalika's face when—
“No…!”
*** squeezed her eyes shut, but that only made the memory clearer, sharper. The wet, final
sounds. The one eye rolling up to show only white. The little sphere of light, her soul, rising
higher and higher and—
"...Mom? Where are you?"
The void swallowed her small, trembling voice without an echo. She turned in a slow,
desperate circle, searching the absolute darkness for any sign of antlers or long black hair or
those gentle hands that made everything make sense again.
Nothing. Just the endless black that used to feel like everything she needed and now felt like
what was left after everything important had been taken away.
Her gaze caught on the only other thing that wasn't darkness or her own reflection: a small
pile of books sitting a few feet away, just as Yhounden had left them. The "better" ones, the
elk had said after the bad day when *** had destroyed all those stupid, lying stories about
beautiful girls getting everything they wanted.
These were quieter stories, with foxes and crows and mice who learned important, simple
lessons about being content with what they were.
Tears came then, hot and steady and silent, nothing like the wild, ugly sobs from the dream,
dripping down from her chin onto the transparent black floor, where they pooled like little
liquid mirrors for a moment before sinking away into nothing.
Her legs folded without her permission, dropping her back into a sitting position on the
smooth floor. When the elk had first found her, this darkness had been the exact right size for
her. Now it stretched too far in every direction, like a dress she’d never grow into.
Read a book.
That's what Mom would say.
Read a book and let yourself get lost inside.Her hand moved without enthusiasm, pulling a thin volume from the top of the pile. The
cover showed a fox reaching for grapes on a high vine, painted in colors that seemed
jarringly, offensively bright against the grey feeling that had settled deep inside her.
*** knew this one—the Kind Elk had read it to her thrice, about wanting things you couldn't
have.
She opened it, and the words swam on the page like drowning ants.
“A fox once saw some high-hanging grapes and wished to eat them..."
Like me….Reaching for something impossible.
Wanting to be good.
Wanting to have a friend.
Wanting what I shouldn’t—
"...he jumped and jumped again but could not reach them—"
*** closed the book with a soft, final *snap* and tossed it listlessly behind her. It landed
somewhere in the dark with a small, muffled thud that the void ate immediately.
Slowly, deliberately, she lay back down. Not curled up for comfort like when the elk used to
stroke her hair and promise wonderful things. Not twisted in pain like in the alley when she'd
tried to tear the monster out of her own skin. Just... flat.
She closed her eyes, though it made no difference in the absolute dark. Her breathing began
to slow, forced, deliberate—
in... out... in... out...
Sleep. Forever.
Let the cold sweat dry.
Let the frantic heart slow until it barely moved at all.
Let this bandages be the last gift she ever received, the last memory made out of something
that might have been love.
And maybe, if was luck was merciful, forget she had ever woken up at all.
Maybe she could forget there had ever been a brave, sad, kind, foolish girl named Agalika
who had, for a few short, impossible hours, believed a monster could choose to be good.
Maybe—
"Yahooo~~~!”A voice, high and bright and utterly incongruous, sliced through the void like a silver bell
through thick fog.
"Your favorite sister is finally here~ ♡ "
Light, hurried footsteps followed, growing louder with each second—*tap-tap-tap-tap*—like
an excited rabbit who'd just found a field of giant carrots, rapidly approaching through the
darkness.
***'s eyes squeezed tighter. Her small fingers curled against the transparent floor, nails
making tiny, useless scratching sounds.
Imagination. It had to be. A phantom sound.
The void was playing cruel tricks now, offering false hope like those mean fairy godmothers
from the stories who gave beautiful gifts that turned to ash and spiders at midnight.
Still, despite herself, a treacherous warmth bloomed in her chest. That familiar, expectant
feeling she always got when Mom would finally return from wherever Moms went when they
left.
Maybe… maybe mom come back?
Maybe she forgave me for the bad dream?
Maybe she’s still willing to teach me how to be good enough?
With a heart tangled in hope and dread, fighting cats locked in a vicious struggle, *** cracked
one eye open just a sliver.
A silhouette approached through the endless dark, small and bouncing with each eager step.
But as the figure drew closer, resolving into focus, the hopeful spark in ***'s chest fizzled
instantly into profound confusion.
It wasn't Mom.
The figure was small instead. Child-sized. Just like her.
*** stared through her barely-open eyes at the impossibility walking towards her.
A perfect mirror, if mirrors could lie and cheat.
The same black jacket and white pleated dress (but pristine and unwrinkled).
The same little Mary Janes (but shining and new).
The same pale, round face with the same wide grey eyes (but grinning with a mischievous
confidence, like the world was made of sugar and delicious secrets just for her).
The same black hair… but cut short, bouncing with each excited skip.How did a human child get here?
Did she follow me from the dream?
One of the orphans?
No. They ran. They called her monster and—
The girl got closer now, and instinctively, *** buried her face back against the floor,
committing fully to playing dead, hoping this strange, loud, impossible creature would
simply pass her by.
The light footsteps stopped directly beside her.
"Nope, the lazy bum is still asleep…"
The human girl mumbled above her, disappointment dripping from her voice like spilled
honey. A loud, theatrical sigh followed shortly.
The footsteps then resumed, but different now. Each Mary Jane hit the nothing-floor with an
exaggerated, deliberate force—
STOMP...
STOMP...
STOMP...
—while the girl hummed a jaunty tune that sounded vaguely like a children's lullaby played
as a military march.
“Geez, and I thought Mynoghra was bad with her ‘just five more eons’ routine!”
The humming resumed, punctuated by more of those heavy steps—
STOMP…
step-step…
STOMP…
—Spiraling inward, closer and closer—
Silence.
